## G. Verification / proof debt (added 2026-07-20)

The SMT/refinement proof layer — Verum's headline feature — had no register presence.

| # | Item | Pri | Anchor | Acceptance |
|---|------|-----|--------|------------|
| G1 | **Refinement checker rejected every satisfiable refinement** — the value-free path asked validity-over-the-base-type (`∀x. P(x)`) instead of inhabitation (`∃x. P(x)`), so `Int{>0}`, `Int{>=0 && <=5}` etc. were all rejected (tell: counterexample OUTSIDE the range). Half-fixed by ROUTE (removed on `verum verify`, left live on `verum check` + LSP). Fix splits the two judgments into one authority; Unknown must not reject an inhabitation claim. | P2 | T0457 (+T0262 runtime asserts) | branch wt/verification-v2 in verify |
| G2 | **One bad axiom disables ALL reflection for a module** — `verify_cmd.rs` hands the reflection registry to Z3 as one string; a single entry naming an undeclared symbol makes Z3 drop the whole block, so the proof status of a theorem depends on unrelated file content (demonstrated: appending one unrelated theorem flipped tactics.vr from 51/9 to 60/1). Root of ~76 of 113 failing core/ proofs. | P1 | T0489 | stable 60/0 + perturbation invariant; branch wt/verification-v2 |
| G3 | **T0354 proof burndown (113 failing core/ proofs)** — decomposed: the `==`-binds-tighter-than-`&&` mis-parse of 19 boolean-algebra laws is FIXED (88629f3d5, count-neutral prerequisite); remainder tracked as T0489 (76, reflection), T0490 (14 higher-order/monad-law), T0491 (18 method/protocol-call), T0480 (result-binder name capture), T0481 (literal-true unproved), T0487 (method-call P-or-not-P). | P2 | T0354 (decomposed) | children burned down + census re-measured post-T0489 |
| G10 | **CLOSED 2026-08-30/31 (3caf36728, 38c0084e2).** ~~Refinement types worked on SCALARS only~~ — `type NonNeg is n: Int where n >= 0` became a solver hypothesis on a parameter and NOT through a record field, so `b.v` handed the solver an unconstrained Int and the counterexample came back EMPTY. The mirror gap sat on the other side of the obligation and was not about refinements at all: a postcondition naming a field of the RESULT could not be discharged even on a record carrying none, because `result` is bound at Int/Bool/Real sort and a record literal is none of those. Until then "the invariant lives in the type and travels with it" held for `Int` and not for `{ x: Int }`. Not a regression — 4 proved / 2 failed on four binaries, the oldest three weeks old. Found by running the registry showcase end to end, which the G-section deadlock repair made possible. | P1 — closed | T0994, T0995; `vcs/specs/L1-core/refinement/` (three specs, one of them the vacuity control) | showcase discharges every proof it states: 6 of 6 |
| G11 | **PARTIALLY CLOSED 2026-08-31 (52dcd32cd, cc479eb97).** A rank-2 type lost its quantifier in the bake — measured to be TWO defects. (a) the field baked as **`Unit`**, not as a placeholder: `resolve_field_type_ref` had no rank-2 arm and fell through to a catch-all returning `Concrete(UNIT)`; the archive now carries `fn<R>(Reducer<B, R>, S) -> Reducer<::Item<I>, R>` and the decoder binds the quantified names per parse. (b) the repro's real root: `try_find_associated_type` returned THE SAME variable for different receivers — the impl's own `B`, unbound because the receiver match cannot reach into a function type's return position. Unbound impl variables are now freshened per query; the repro and `core-tests/base/iterator/unit_test.vr` are clean and 225 type-system specs moved ZERO verdicts. STILL OPEN because the negative control fails: a single call with a MISMATCHED element type typechecks clean, and did before the fix too — that parameter has never been checked. The cure is named and costed: the archive carries no generic-parameter list for impls (`params=0 bounds=[]` for every one), so the recovery stage is unreachable for the whole baked stdlib. | P1 | T0997 | negative control added to acceptance: a mismatched single call must be REFUSED |
| G13 | **`a < b` on a heap value returns a NONDETERMINISTICALLY WRONG answer** — seven lines, no attributes: two records with `v: 1` and `v: 2` print `true` in ~70% of runs and `false` in the rest, same binary, same source, and every run a bytecode-cache HIT. Two records with EQUAL fields never report "neither is less": `lt` and `gt` are always opposite and which one is true flips — the signature of comparing ADDRESSES. `handle_lti` does `va.as_integer_compatible() < vb.as_integer_compatible()` with no guard for a pointer operand, while `==` goes through `deep_value_eq` and is correct. Lists flip too; strings are unaffected and stable. | P0 | T1017 | equal fields report neither less nor greater; unequal report a stable order; strings stay correct |
| G14 | **`@derive(...)` is never applied** — `ProtocolChecker::derive` (which dispatches `"Ord" => derive_ord`) has ZERO callers, and `DerivedBody` — the shape those functions produce — is read nowhere outside its own file. The attribute is registered, so no "unknown attribute" is reported: it parses and is dropped. `@derive(Eq)` goes unnoticed because `==` is structural anyway; `@derive(Ord)` is what makes it visible, via G13. | P1 | T1018 | `@derive(Ord)` produces an Ord impl AND a type without it behaves differently |
| G15 | **A deprecated feature and the replacement its own help names both evaluate to `nil`** — `const N` generics warn "use meta parameters instead, example `N: meta <type>`", and `<N: meta Int>` produces the same `nil` at runtime with `verum check` clean. One pass already knows: "refinement reflection: skipping `capacity` — its body references `N`". Every spec with `<const ` is `@test: parse-pass`, so runtime behaviour was covered nowhere. | P1 | T1015 | both forms compute the parameter or are refused; the spec runs, not just parses |
| G16 | **A malformed `provide` crashes the compiler instead of diagnosing** — `provide Clock = Clock { now: … }` (a record literal where an implementing type belongs) passes `verum check` and then aborts with an internal error and a crash report, after naming a FOREIGN type guessed from 27 field-name candidates. The refusal is right; its form is not, and this is the first mistake anyone makes after reading about contexts. | P2 | T1016 | the same input gets a diagnostic with a span; no crash report |
| G17 | **Async is EAGER in the interpreter** — an `async fn` body runs at the CALL SITE, so there is no future to await. Measured by effect order, not by an error: `print("before"); let f = effect("A"); print("after-call")` emits *before / body A / after-call*, where a deferred call would emit *before / after-call / body A*. Three symptoms, one root: calling an async fn returns the VALUE; `.await` on it fails with "Cannot await non-future type: Int" (true by the letter, misleading by the sense — there is nothing to await); and `spawn { … }` returns an `Int`, so `.join()` panics on a TaskHandle method. `verum check` is clean for all three. The cost beyond the errors: a program relying on `spawn` for parallelism runs SERIALLY and silently. Tier 0 only; AOT unmeasured. | P1 | T1020 | an async fn returns a future (body does not run until `.await`); `spawn` returns a handle; effect order becomes before / after-call / body A |
| G18 | **Half the iterator terminators panic after ANY adapter** — `xs.iter().map(…).find(…)` dies with "method 'next' not found", check clean, while `fold`/`collect`/`count` on the same chain are fine. TWO defects under one word: (A) `any/all/find/position` — the default IS entered and its inner `next` gets a receiver that is not a pointer at all (raw bits like 0x58d1); root MEASURED — `handler=Call` does not deliver generic witnesses while `CallG` does (calls.rs, the only difference between them), so a generic adapter method reached by a static call cannot resolve its inner iterator. (B) `last/nth/max` — the receiver is CORRECT and the method itself is not found. Uncovered remainder of the closed P0 T0729, whose acceptance was one example with one terminator while its title claimed all of them. | P1 | T1021 | every terminator named individually in the acceptance, on base and after each adapter |
| G19 | **An explicit fully-qualified `mount` does not resolve, and falls back to a bare name under first-wins** — measured on a FRESH two-file user cog, not on core/. `mount probe_cog.util.math.{Secret}` bound the STDLIB `Secret`: `field 'v' not found … Available members: [as_text, created_at, data, expires_at, …]`. With a unique name the program runs, and the compiler prints what it did: `[mount-fallback] explicit mount '…ProbeBox' did not resolve to its named path; binding bare 'ProbeBox' owned by a first-wins declarer`. The user wrote the most explicit form the language offers and still got someone else's type; the only defence today is not to name anything as the stdlib does. | P1 | T1022 | the named path binds ITS type; no mount-fallback warning |
| G20 | **Visibility modifiers restrict nothing** — from another module, a bare `fn`, an `internal fn`, a non-public type AND its fields all mount and run, check clean (prints 2, 3, 7 on the probe). The grammar carries `public`, `internal`, `protected` and `private`, and even `public(cog)`, `public(super)` and `public(in path)`, and `docs/by-example/10-mount-system` explains re-exports as the way to keep "a stable public surface while the internal file layout changes" — the whole point of which is the inside/outside distinction. Today every internal helper is part of the external contract, and its author does not know it. Likely one root with G19: if binding goes by bare name AROUND the module surface, there is nobody left to ask about that surface's visibility. | P1 | T1023 | a non-public name is REFUSED across modules; public/internal/private differ in behaviour, not only in parsing |
| G12 | **`verum explain` can answer about the WRONG code** — `error<E0203>` is printed and `verum explain E203` describes a missing module. Two spellings are live (`Exxx` in the error registry, `E0xxx` in the diagnostics explanations and compiler lints) and where their digits coincide the meanings do not: 51 four-digit codes, 41 with a colliding twin. A coverage gate asking "is every printed code registered?" is GREEN on all 41 — both codes are registered, as different things. One instance fixed outright (E801, printed for an undeclared context and registered as an FFI ABI mismatch, now E613). | P1 | T0996; ratcheted at 41 by `scripts/ci/check_error_code_namespaces.py` | one namespace; the ratchet's baseline reaches 0 |
| G5 | **CLOSED 2026-08-30 (f66d18a41).** ~~THE VERIFIER PROVES A POSTCONDITION THE PROGRAM VIOLATES~~ — `ensures result == 0` reports `✓ Proved` for a function the interpreter runs to `-1`, with a live-obligation control (`ensures result == 999` Fails). A postcondition is discharged by asserting `result == body`; a call renders as `(name args…)` keyed on the NAME, so two calls to one impure function are one term and congruence gives 0. Correct for a pure function — the pure twin still proves, and the two-argument variant correctly fails. Three layers: the property inferrer does not treat a `static mut` as state (so `pure fn tick()` is ACCEPTED); `verify_cmd` asserts the body equation unasked; and the purity gate that would answer, `is_reflectable`, has four callers, all its own unit tests. Its doc comment says the properties "will be derived from the type checker's PropertySet analysis" — future tense. The soundness argument in `fold_let_bindings` cites the CONTEXT gate, and contexts are not properties. Measured after: `drift` ✗ Failed, the pure twin ✓ Proved, `pure fn tick()` E503 Mutates+WritesExternal+ReadsExternal. | **P0 — closed** | T0982; pinned by a PAIR under `vcs/specs/L0-critical/verification/two_calls_to_one_{impure,pure}_function_*` | the impure spec fails, the pure twin keeps proving, and the argument in `fold_let_bindings` names the gate that actually holds it |
| G6 | **CLOSED 2026-08-30 (f66d18a41).** ~~`pure` is enforced or not depending on DECLARATION ORDER~~ — `pure fn caller() { helper() }` above `fn helper() { print("x"); 1 }` is ACCEPTED; the same two lines swapped are E503. Properties are registered as the walker goes, in source order, and the `Call` arm treats a callee it cannot find as contributing nothing, so a forward reference reads as pure. The module walk already registers function SIGNATURES in a phase of its own precisely so forward references resolve; properties never got that phase. Remedy shared with G5: one fixpoint seeding pass per module before bodies are checked. Measured after: callee above, below and two hops all E503. | P1 — closed | T0985 | the swapped file is refused; the corpus census reports what the change newly refuses |
| G7 | **CLOSED 2026-08-31 (ae93432b7, fbbf517f3).** ~~`pure fn f() using [Random]` is accepted~~ — the positive-context half landed first; the SECOND half was found by the same probe and was worse: a negative context spelled as a GROUP (`using Pure = [!IO, !Random]`) was read as POSITIVE, because the group's own reference carries `is_negative: false` and the gate read the NAME instead of looking through it. One rule, two spellings of the same thing, two answers. The gate now walks to the first positive LEAF and names both ("declares context `Random` through group `Mixed`"); a group mixing a promise with an injection is still refused. | P2 | T0986 | done |
| G9 | **CLOSED 2026-08-30 (bd2568ad9).** ~~A NEGATIVE context group reads as REQUIRED~~ — `using Pure = [!IO, !Random]` on a callee makes its caller report `E801: context IO used but not declared`. `build_function_contexts_map` expands a group and inserts every member as required, dropping the negation; three lines above it, the same check IS performed for a context written inline, and `ContextRef` has carried `is_negative` all along. Dated by measurement across three binaries (2026-08-25: 0 errors on a four-line probe; twice today: 2). Reachable only since the T0977 repair made the context collector walk a block's TAIL expression, so a call in tail position first reached the T0239 context-forwarding arm — the defect is older than that repair, which merely exposed it. Reddens the purity suite's OK-half. Root: `ContextRequirement::new` sets `is_negative: false`, so the flag was dropped AT CONSTRUCTION and no consumer could see it — two correct-looking patches to consumers measured inert first. | P1 — closed | T0991 | the four-line probe checks clean AND a genuinely missing context still raises the error |
| G8 | **A spec named `pure_function_validation.vr` REQUIRED that purity violations be accepted** — its header listed four "(FAIL)" sections and it carried twelve functions named `impure_*`, each written to be refused, under a file directive of `@test: typecheck-pass`. Measured: the compiler refuses three of the twelve. Distinct from §0-style false-green: this one measures, and demands the wrong answer, so while green it certified the gap as intended. Split 2026-08-30 into three green files and three red ones, each red carrying its task. | P1 | T0982/T0984/T0986; `vcs/specs/L1-core/types/pure/` | the three red halves go green as their tasks land; no file mixes a working refusal with a broken one (a `typecheck-fail` spec passes on any single error) |
| G4 | **209 verification tests have NEVER compiled** — 7 test files carry a file-level `#![cfg(feature = "X")]` where X is declared in no Cargo.toml, so the gate never fires and the file is silently dropped from the build: 4820 lines / 209 `#[test]` fns that have never once run. **Six of the seven are `verum_smt`** — dependent_types 79, comprehensive 31 (old Z3 API), advanced_features 26, dependent_types_v2 23, model_extraction 13 (old Z3 API), tensor_refinement 11 — plus verum_integration_tests/end_to_end 26. So effective SMT/refinement coverage is materially below what the tree advertises, which is the same false-green this section was created to surface. 2026-07-25: gates rewritten to `#![cfg(any())]` with in-file reasons so the exclusion stops masquerading as an opt-in flag (6dcc99640) — that is honesty, NOT a fix. (T0632) | P1 | T0632; `crates/verum_smt/tests/*` | per file: revived (compiles, runs, passes, gate deleted) or deleted with its intent recorded |

---

Cross-references: `docs/architecture/multi-session-taskpool.md` (the work
queue this register feeds — `scripts/taskpool/tp list open`),
`docs/architecture/tier-coherence-pillars.md` (P1–P5
pillar plan retiring §40/§46/#27), `docs/architecture/intrinsic-dispatch-contract.md`
(Rules 4–5 open work), `core-tests/INVENTORY.md` (per-module truth),
memory session file `session_2026-07-09_tracing_analysis_and_docs.md`
(18 toolchain-constraint classes with repros).

**Roots found 2026-09-01, and they fall into three mechanisms rather than
one.** The observable is always the same — accepted in silence — and the
cause is in a different place each time. The distinction is worth keeping
because it predicts the COST:

| Task | Mechanism | Root | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| T1025 | machinery exists, nobody asks | `Parser::new` instead of `with_attr_validation`; and `take_attr_warnings` has ZERO callers in production code — the defect is two floors deep | a call, then a warning channel through an API whose every caller expects `Result<Module, errors>` |
| T1029 | machinery exists, nobody asks | conformance compares NAME sets; the protocol registry holds the full declaration one line below where the map drops the signatures. SWEPT 2026-09-01 over all 2560 core/ files, 0 mute, 267 messages — and EVERY identified class is a comparison artefact (variant expansion, alias pairs, payloads rendered as type arguments by the normaliser itself, reference qualifiers lost because the two sides come from DIFFERENT builders). Zero defects demonstrated; the instrument is not yet able to show one | both sides from one builder; alias resolution; compare a normalised variant by head — then a number is worth producing |
| T1026 | the input is destroyed at the door | a function's `decreases` measure is parsed into `let _expr` and thrown away; `FunctionDecl` has no field for it | 112 construction sites, 61 of them tests, no `Default` to lean on |
| T1027 | a quantity is never computed | CORRECTED 2026-09-01: not a missing check. `VERUM_TRACE_CTXCALL` shows the accounting sees `contexts=None` for the function that USES the context — correctly, it declares none — and emits no line at all for the context call itself. The system tracks DECLARATIONS and PROPAGATION and never computes USAGE, so there is no value for a check to consult | compute a new quantity (contexts a body touches), with `provide` in the same function subtracting from it |
| T1028 | the wrong question is asked | both branches printed `ui::success`; "did errors appear" was asked, "did the DECLARED errors appear" was not | five lines — LANDED f2794378b |
| T1023 | the input is destroyed at the door | CORRECTED 2026-09-01: the check exists (`is_visible_from_path`), IS called on the named-import path (imports.rs:600), and handles all seven grammar forms — refusing when the module path is unknown rather than guessing. The defect is upstream: `Resolver::new` hardcodes `visibility: Visibility::Public`, and `with_visibility`, which takes it, has ONE caller outside tests. Every item claims to be public | carry the declared modifier from the AST to the export entry; the seven forms are already implemented |

**The measurement discipline that produced these, stated once because it
was violated three times:** every blast-radius estimate made by reading
code was wrong — by factors of six, seven and seventeen. The numbers that
held were taken from the compiler itself, and every census filter was
checked against known-positive and known-negative cases before its number
was reported.

## H. Distinctive capabilities — claimed vs. measured (2026-08-31)

Verum's argument is not that it has types, loops and generics. It is the
short list of things its neighbours do not do: laws the compiler
enforces, effects in the signature, sizes in the type, a size of proof
per function. Those are what a reader evaluates the language on, and
until today none of them had a measured verdict in one place — each was
either prose in a guide, a chapter in the registry showcase, or a
sentence beside a grammar production.

Every row below was measured on a release binary on 2026-08-31, each
with a control: a WORKS row means the correct form passes AND the
incorrect form is refused; a BROKEN row means the incorrect form is
accepted, or the correct one is refused. A row with only one half
measured says so.

**Read this table before writing documentation or a showcase chapter.**
Four capabilities on this list are inert, and prose asserting otherwise
already existed for three of them.

| Capability | Verdict | Measured | Task |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Purity / computational properties** — a `pure fn` may not do I/O, spawn, or mutate through `&mut` | **WORKS** (order-dependence landed fixed 2026-08-31, e0ce1a95e) | four E503 forms refused, one at a time, and gated in the registry showcase — the call case used to be refused only when the callee was declared ABOVE its caller — moving it below made main accept the same program, and that is now fixed on main. Bounded by measurement: E310, E400 and `spawn`-in-`pure` all still fire with declarations moved below | T0985, T1024 |
| **Affine and linear types** — affine is used at most once, linear exactly once | **WORKS** | E310 on second use; gated | — |
| **Sizes in the type** — `Digest<32>` and `Digest<8>` are different types | **WORKS** | E400 "expected '32', found '8'"; gated | — |
| **Refinement types on scalars** — `type NonNeg is n: Int where n >= 0` | **WORKS** | hypothesis reaches the solver on a parameter | G10 |
| **Refinements through record fields** | **WORKS** (landed 2026-08-31, e0ce1a95e) | `versions.vr` went from 4 proved / 2 failed to 6/0. Measured on both binaries WITH the over-proving control: the false postcondition `ensures result.v < b.v` stays refused, so the fix did not simply make the solver credulous | T0994, T0995, T1024 |
| **Three-tier references (CBGR)** — `&T`, `&checked T`, `&unsafe T` | **WORKS** | a reference that escapes its scope is refused at tier 0 AND tier 1 with `error<E312>: \`x\` does not live long enough`; a correct borrow through `&checked` compiles and runs. The headline memory-safety claim is the one distinctive feature measured today that holds in both directions without qualification | — |
| **Ghost contracts** — `@ghost requires` | **WORKS** | used as a hypothesis (the correct body proves), and it does NOT weaken the conclusion: the same body violating `ensures result >= 0` fails with the ghost precondition exactly as it does without it. The risk under test — a modifier that silently disables checking — is not present | — |
| **Loop termination** under `@verify(thorough)` | **WORKS** | a `while` whose counter never changes reports `1 proved, 1 FAILED` | T0671 |
| **Loop invariant preservation** | **WORKS** | a false invariant is refused with a counterexample naming the breaking state | — |
| **Postcondition proved FROM a loop invariant** | **BROKEN** | `result` is unconstrained after a loop — fails even where the loop is not entered (`n = 0`); bisected to one token: `u + 1` proves, `u + i` and `u + k` do not | T0905 |
| **Recursion termination** — a declared `decreases` measure | **NOT CHECKED** | a measure that INCREASES verifies clean under `check`, `verify`, `@verify(thorough)` and `@total` | T1026 |
| **Protocol axioms as implement-site obligations** — trait laws the compiler enforces | **INERT** | a violating implementation, a conforming one, and a `proof` clause naming an axiom that does not exist all produce identical output | T0989 |
| **Attribute validation** | **UNWIRED** | a typo in `@verify(thorough)` — name or argument — turns a FAILING verification green with no diagnostic; the validator exists and the compile path never constructs it | T1025 |
| **Contexts in the signature** — a function that uses a capability must name it | **NOT ENFORCED** | a function with no `using [Clock]` reaches the clock, compiles, runs, and returns a value of an unrelated type **NEEDS RE-MEASURING (2026-09-04):** an observation from another probe points the other way. `fn who() -> Text using [Greeter]` called from a `main` that declares nothing is REFUSED — `error<E613>: context 'Greeter' used but not declared in function signature`, with both helps offered. That is the transitive direction; this row's measurement is the direct one (a body reaching `Clock` with no `using`). Both can hold at once, and nobody has asked which is which since the context-validation phase was wired into `check` (eaa332813, a90449a4d). Re-run the row's own probe before quoting it. | T1027 |
| **Const generic values at run time** | **BROKEN** | the type-level distinction works (row 3); reading the parameter as a value yields `nil`, and the recommended `meta` replacement does too | T1015 |
| **Rank-2 function types** — `fn<R>(...)` as a record field | **BROKEN** | the field bakes as `Unit` | T0997, G11 |
| **Visibility modifiers** | **RESTRICT NOTHING** | from another module a bare `fn`, an `internal fn`, a non-public type and its fields all mount and run | T1023 |
| **Protocol conformance** — `implement Protocol for T` | **NAMES ONLY** | a missing method is refused with E405, and a method whose signature differs entirely from the protocol's is accepted; a generic function bounded by the protocol then passes an `Int` into a `Text` parameter and returns the `Bool` it gets back where it promised an `Int` **NARROWED 2026-09-04 (T1073, 16509c822):** the signature half is no longer wholly absent. The RETURN position has been checked since T1078, and the PARAMETER position is now checked on the same terms — a pair that admits no benign reading, with both sides resolved through the alias table so `NonNeg` and `Int` compare equal while `Text` against `NonNeg` does not. Measured: `fn take(n: Text)` against `fn take(n: NonNeg)` is E427, `fn take(n: Int)` stays clean (parameters are contravariant), 163 core/ carrier files report zero. What remains unchecked is the middle ground the rule deliberately declines — two NAMED types that might be an alias pair — and arity, which the comparison states it will not guess at. **AND THE FIRST CLAUSE NEEDS RE-MEASURING:** this row says a missing method is refused with E405, and a probe on 2026-09-04 with a LOCALLY declared protocol — `implement Sink for Box` providing `nosuch` instead of `take` — was accepted in silence. Both may be true if E405 fires only for protocols loaded from the archive; nobody has asked which. | T1029 |
| **Generic associated types** — `type Item<T>` in a protocol | **ARITY ENFORCED 2026-09-03; RESULT TYPE STILL UNENFORCED** | the arity half is closed (937c139bf): binding `type Out = Int` against a declared `type Out<T>` is refused with E405, and the correct binding is accepted — before the fix the comparison ran BACKWARDS, accepting the violation and refusing the correct program, because every associated type was registered through a constructor that hardcodes an empty type-param list. The remaining half is the row above's defect seen through GATs: an implementation may bind `Item<T> = Maybe<T>` and return `List<Int>` from the method that uses it | T1073 (arity), T1029 (result type) | **THE UNENFORCED HALF HAS A MECHANISM (T1073, 2026-09-04):** `check_gat_instantiation` (`verum_types/src/infer/modules.rs`) implements exactly it — synthesis mode infers the instantiation, checking mode verifies the concrete arguments satisfy the declared bounds — and has ZERO callers, in src and in tests alike. No live counterpart exists under another name: a grep for check/verify/validate_*(hkt|gat|associated|projection)* in production finds only negative-bound and negative-context checks, a different subject. So this row's remaining half is not unwritten, it is unasked.
| **Row polymorphism** — `{ x: Int \| r }` as a parameter type | **PARSES, CONSTRAINS NOTHING** | the type is grammatical (`verum.ebnf:1403`) and a `Point` passed to `fn dist<r>(p: { x: Float, y: Float \| r })` is accepted — but so is a record carrying NEITHER field. The expression half does not exist: anonymous record braces parse as a BLOCK (`E100 unbound variable`), the documented pipe-splat `{ a: 5 \| ..base }` parses as a bitwise-or with a range (`E400 expected 'Range<P>'`), and the lacks predicate `where r # y` is a parse error. The comma spelling `P { a: 5, ..base }` works | T1073 |
| **`@multiversion`** — runtime CPU dispatch on a hot function | **PARSED, CONSUMED BY NOTHING** | measured 2026-09-04: `MultiversionAttr` has no consumer in the tree; `ifunc`, `target_clones`, `cpu_dispatch` and any resolver emission are absent from codegen; the only CPUID in the workspace reads a device vendor string in the interpreter. `@multiversion(zzq_nonsense = "x")` checks clean, and `MultiversionAttr::from_attribute` — which would reject the ten BARE uses in `core/simd/bytes.vr` — is never called. Six places asserted the capability in the indicative and were corrected (7db397316 + the website's two, one of which invented a "~3 ns dispatch cost"). | T1073 |
| **`@must_handle`** — a Result whose error type must be handled | **THE PARSER DOES NOT KNOW THE ATTRIBUTE** | measured 2026-09-04: a function marked `@must_handle` whose Result is dropped gives `warning<W0400>: unknown attribute`. Behind that non-existent attribute stand five layers: `check_must_handle_result`, `MustHandleRegistry`, `AnnotationRegistry`, a 1288-line flow-sensitive analysis (`verum_types/src/control_flow.rs`, whose only consumer is a re-export), error code E0317 in the registry, and a full explanation with examples in `verum_diagnostics`. Nothing in `docs/` or the website promises it, so no page is wrong. The two halves disagree about the subject — the explanation puts the attribute on a FUNCTION, the registry keys on an ERROR TYPE — so wiring it is a language decision, not debt. | T1073 |
| **`@specialize`** — a more specific impl overrides a general one | **PARSED, DROPPED AT THE REGISTRY** | measured 2026-09-04: `decls.rs` builds `ProtocolImpl` at two sites and both write `specialization: Maybe::None`, so the attribute never reaches the record `validate_specializations` reads. That validator — "the main entry point for specialization validation" — has no production caller; of its four clauses only overlap detection is live elsewhere (`detect_overlaps`, 4 callers), while `check_specificity`, `validate_specificity` and `check_lattice` have zero between them. `@specialize` on an impl with NOTHING to specialise is accepted in silence; probe zero confirms the position can speak (`@zzznotathing` gives W0400). `ImplDecl` already carries `attributes`, so the first clause is a read nobody does. | T1073 |
| **Termination of MUTUAL recursion** | **DIRECT CHECKED, MUTUAL NOT** | measured 2026-09-04: `pure fn spin(n) { spin(n) }` is refused with `error<E321>: potential stack overflow: unbounded recursion detected` under both `check` and `verify`; `ping(n) { pong(n) }` beside `pong(n) { ping(n) }` checks clean and `verum verify` reports "Proved". The live walk looks for calls to `func_name` INSIDE `func_name`, so it can only see the direct case. `TerminationError::MutualRecursionCycle` exists with its message, `check_mutual_recursion` implements the DFS, and the call graph it needs IS populated on the live path (`termination.rs:503`) — nothing calls it. MEASURED 2026-09-04 over 163 core/ carriers with the detector behind a trace: 248 reports, GENUINE MUTUAL CYCLES **ZERO** — every one is a self-loop (`simple_query`, `constraint_to_range`, `dual`, `matches_impl`), and two of those files compile with no error at all (`core/cog/resolve.vr`, `core/database/postgres/connection.vr`: 0 errors, 0 E321) while the detector flags them 42 and 70 times. And the controls settle it outright: `ping`/`pong` with NO base case reports 2, and the same shape WITH a base case and a decreasing argument reports **2 as well** — the detector cannot tell a terminating mutual recursion from a non-terminating one. It detects cycles and has no notion of a decreasing measure. The fix is therefore NOT to wire it but to extend the live walk, which already understands measures ("recursive call to `X` has no decreasing argument", "invalid decreasing clause") and only lacks a path across the call graph. Acceptance for that work must take its TRUE pole from a spec — core/ contains no genuine mutual cycle to test against. | T1073 |
| **A protocol impl's PARAMETER types** | **FIXED 2026-09-04** | `implement Sink for Box { fn take(n: Text) }` against `fn take(n: NonNeg)` compiled with zero diagnostics: T1078 gave the RETURN position a rule admitting no benign reading and left the parameters on the older scalar-only one. Closed in 16509c822 by resolving both sides through the alias table rather than excluding names — the exclusion silenced the true pole with the false one. Acceptance: four poles, 200-file ratchet, and 163 files drawn from the 973 core/ carriers of `implement` with a positive control at 1. | T1073 |
| **Tagged literals** — `url#"..."`, `ip#"..."`, `sql#"..."` | **TYPED, NOT VALIDATED** | the type half works and is checked (`takes_int(url#"...")` is refused with `found 'Uri'`, control `found 'Text'`), but the content is not: `url#"ht!tp:// not a url ??"`, `ip#"999.999.999.999"`, `d#"not-a-date"`, `ver#"not-a-version"`, `rx#"[unclosed"`, `sql#"!!! not sql @@@"` and `json#"{ this is not json ,,, }"` all compile clean. The only rejection observed is the lexer's bracket balance. Six of the documented result-type names were also wrong, and `json5#` produces plain `Text` — no distinct type at all | T1073 |
| **Tensor shapes in the type** — `Tensor<F32, [2, 3]>` | **NOT CARRIED; and no tensor literal survives execution** | the annotation resolves to `DynTensor<F32, [Int; 3]>` — rank survives, SIZES do not, and `verum check` accepts `tensor<2,3> + tensor<5,4>` with zero diagnostics where the public page promises a broadcast error. At run time the shape question is unreachable: `let a = tensor<2,3> Float32 { };` ALONE dies with `internal compiler error (worker thread panicked): Expected int, got Some(0)` — with or without a filled body, with or without arithmetic; a file with no tensor runs fine. My first write-up said "the same-shape control compiles and runs" — the control had been verified under `check` and reported under `run`, and running it produced the same ICE. Also: three earlier probes measured NOTHING, because `Tensor.zeros()` unifies with anything (`let x: Int = Tensor.zeros()` accepted) and a `Tensor<…>` parameter accepts a plain `Int` | T1073 |
| **Capability attenuation** — `T with [Read]` at a call site | **FIXED 2026-09-03** | both directions were refused, and the NARROWING — the give-away direction the feature exists for — was reported as "capability cannot be widened". The lattice rule (`ReadWrite` grants `ReadOnly`/`WriteOnly`, `Admin` grants all) existed hand-written in ONE of three sites; it is now a single authority (`TypeCapabilitySet::grants`) asked by all three. Measured after: `[ReadWrite]` where `[Read]` is required accepted, `[Read]` where `[ReadWrite]` is required still E411 | T1073 |
| **A refutable pattern in a parameter** — `fn f(E.K(x): E)` | **REFUSED 2026-09-03; bare form still accepted** | `verum check` was clean and the interpreter returned `Variant(27149)` from a function declared `-> Int` — a wrong value of the wrong type, silently. `destructuring.md` stated the rule; a grep for `irrefutab\|is_refutable` found COMMENTS ONLY. Now E429 on the QUALIFIED spelling; a bare `Some(x)` is still accepted, deliberately, because distinguishing a newtype unwrap (`UserId(n)`) needs the resolved type and the first predicate produced three false positives in one spec file | T1073 |
| **Awaiting a handle twice** | **ACCEPTED** | `let h = spawn { 1 }; h.await; h.await;` compiles, with the single-await control accepted too — so a future is not consumed by awaiting, and `pending.iter()` after a by-value loop would also be accepted | T1020 |
| **Structured concurrency** — `async fn`, `.await`, `spawn`, `join` | **ERASED AT THE CALL SITE** | a call to `async fn f() -> Int` has type `Int`, not a future over it — the compiler says so itself ("Cannot await non-future type: Int"). `spawn{}.join()` passes `check` and panics at run time; `join(a, b)` returns a variant of an unrelated type rather than failing | T1020 |
| **A file that declares it must fail** — `@expected-error` in a conformance spec | **NOT ENFORCED** | when the declared error does not appear, `verum check` prints an ordinary success line and exits 0 — the same exit code as when it does appear | T1028 |
| **An explicit mount path binds what it names** | **BROKEN** | `mount a.b.{Secret}` falls back to the bare name and binds the stdlib's `Secret` | T1022, T0782 |

**The pattern across the broken rows, and it is one pattern.** Nine of
the sixteen fail in the same direction: an input the compiler cannot
honour is counted in favour of the thing being checked. An unknown
attribute, an absent axiom, a growing measure and an undeclared context
all produce *success*, not silence. That property now has a gate of its
own — `scripts/ci/check_silent_acceptance.py`, a ratchet carrying one
row per instance plus a positive control — so the fifth instance is
caught without anyone having to think of it first.

| A57 | **A NAME DECLARED INSIDE `@ffi(...) extern { }` IS EXPORTED AND CALLABLE-REGISTERED NOWHERE** (2026-09-04, T1085) — `core/io/fs.vr` mounts six names from `core/sys/darwin/libsystem.vr` in ONE statement. `errno`, `DIR` and `Dirent` resolve; `opendir`, `readdir` and `closedir` give `error<E100>: unbound variable`. The first three are declared OUTSIDE the FFI block (:1109, :819, :824), the last three inside it (:39-649, 117 declarations). Same file, same statement, same platform — the cleanest positive/negative pair in the tree. | — | — | — |

THE CHAIN, every link located in source:

    core_compiler.rs:205        adds the name to the module's EXPORT set
                                (only if `public` — which is why someone
                                wrote `public` on exactly those three)
    vbc/codegen/mod.rs:12870    puts it in `module.ffi_symbols`,
                                NOT in `module.functions`
    archive_metadata.rs         reads `module.types` and
                                `module.functions`; never `ffi_symbols`
    -> `meta.functions`         has no entry
    path_resolution.rs:815      `ctx.env.lookup(name)` -> None
    -> E100

**Two registries that disagree and nothing compares them.** A name is
exported and callable-registered nowhere; the failure is silent at every
layer and surfaces only at a use site in another file.

SIZED: 12 live instances in 3 files, and all three are already in
`scripts/ci/core_compile_known_failures.txt` —

    core/mem/allocator.vr    mmap_ffi, munmap, mprotect, madvise
    core/mem/segment.vr      mmap_ffi, munmap, mprotect, madvise
    core/sys/fs_watch.vr     kqueue, kevent, open, close

The first two are the ALLOCATOR's page path. A known-failures baseline is
where a finding goes to stop being a finding.

THE FIRST COUNT WAS 29 AND KEYED ON THE WRONG THING: every `public fn`
inside any extern block anywhere, then a grep for mounts of those NAMES.
That counts `fs.vr` mounting `read`/`open`/`write`/`close`, which it does
under `@cfg(target_os = "linux")` from `core.sys.linux.syscall` where
they are ordinary functions. Resolving the MODULE from the mount path
first: 29 -> 12.

FIX SITE: `register_module_metadata` should build a descriptor per
`ffi_symbols` entry — `FfiSymbol` already carries `signature`, so nothing
needs inventing. `wrapper_fn` looks like a half-built shortcut and is
not: its only write anywhere is `None` in the constructor.

DO NOT "JUST BUILD THE DESCRIPTOR", and here is the reason rather than
the instruction. `register_ffi_extern_function`
(verum_vbc/src/codegen/mod.rs:10690) assigns `FunctionId(u32::MAX)` and
says why in a comment: an extern function DELIBERATELY has no function
id, because callers detect it through `ffi_function_map` and emit
`FfiExtended` against the FFI SYMBOL id, never `Call`. Appending a
FunctionDescriptor to `module.functions` gives it an id, and a
cross-module caller resolving through the archive then emits a `Call`
to an id with no bytecode — trading `E100: unbound variable` for
`Function 2147483647 not found`, which is strictly worse and far harder
to attribute afterwards.

I reached that plan through three earlier reads that each looked
finished — the consumer said "translate CType", the producer said
"build it upstream where the names survive", the marker question said
"`bytecode_length > 0` already exists" — and only opening the registrar
refuted it. The registrar was the LAST call in the very loop being
read, with a name that said what it did.

So the fix has two halves and neither is a descriptor: the archive must
carry the module's FFI SYMBOLS (name, library, signature) so a consumer
can rebuild the equivalent of `ffi_function_map`, and name resolution
must bind such a name with a marker that steers the call site to
`FfiExtended`.

WORKAROUND IN PLACE (83c1c5d12): three `safe_*` wrappers outside the
block, so `read_dir` works on macOS. Five sites now pay this tax
locally, which is the argument for the root: the workaround is cheap
ONCE and the cost is that nobody ever pays for the root.

| A60 | **THE CBGR DANGLING CHECK HAD NO TRUE POSITIVES — its entire output was false** (2026-09-04, T1102, 6ada2105a) — `E312 dangling reference detected` came out of three independently healthy programs AND one genuine use-after-free, identical down to the raw fields (`ref=RefId(1) at=BlockId(0) span=NONE`). One unsound constraint produced all of it: the successor-edge rule demanded a lifetime be live at the PREDECESSOR of the block that DEFINES it, so every `&` in a function with more than one basic block qualified. | — | — | — |
| A61 | **ON macOS THE RUNTIME'S ABORT PATH IS UNREACHABLE — a failed `verum_init()` proceeds into `unreachable()`.** `core/sys/init.vr:623` is the failure arm of runtime initialisation: — match super.verum_init() { Result.Ok(()) => {} Result.Err(_) => { sys.darwin.libsystem.exit(1); unreachable() } } — `exit` is declared `fn exit(status: Int32) -> !;` INSIDE the `@ffi(...) extern { }` block at `core/sys/darwin/libsystem.vr:197`, and a name declared inside such a block is exported but registered callable nowhere — the T1085 class. So the qualified cross-module call does not resolve (`error<E100>: unbound variable: sys.darwin.libsystem.exit`) and the process cannot exit. **A CONTROL I OFFERED HERE WAS FALSE AND IS WITHDRAWN.** I wrote that `pthread_exit` at :279 is `public` and OUTSIDE the block, making it the resolving counterpart to `exit`. Both are inside — the block spans :39-649 — and I never checked the line number against the block bounds, which is the single fact such a pair depends on. Worse, `core/sys/darwin/thread.vr:42` mounts `pthread_self`, `pthread_create`, `pthread_join` from that block and carries **18 E100 diagnostics on exactly those names**, so the supposed control is itself a victim. The real discriminator is the one verum-2b had already established from the other side: `errno` is declared OUTSIDE the block and resolves, while everything inside does not. — WHY THIS IS NOT MERELY A COMPILE ERROR: the E100 is how the defect became visible, not what it is. A runtime that fails to initialise on macOS falls through its abort into a state the code declares impossible. T1085's cost was previously framed as "five sites pay a local tax", which reads as workarounds being cheap; this site is not a tax, it is a missing abort. — NO PORTABLE ROUTE AROUND IT: `core/sys/mod.vr:273` does re-export `exit`, but from `.linux.syscall`, and this is the macOS branch. The repair is a `safe_exit` wrapper outside the extern block plus the call site — the same shape as the opendir/readdir/closedir wrappers — and is held by the session working T1085. **RESOLVED 2026-09-04 17:13 (v37): the abort path is restored.** verum-2b added a `safe_exit` wrapper outside the extern block and rewrote the call site; `core/sys/init.vr` goes 1 -> 0, the removed diagnostic being `unbound variable: exit`. `verum_init()`'s failure branch now calls a bound name instead of falling through to `unreachable()`, and the wrapper was verified present in the archive beside `safe_getcwd` as an independently-working control before the number was read. The remaining 12 files of the class are untouched. — (Before that:) The file stayed in `core_compile_known_failures.txt` until it landed, deliberately: a gate going red on a file everyone expects repaired within the hour teaches people to ignore the gate. — **THE WHOLE POPULATION, COUNTED: 28 WRAPPED, 10 RAW.** Cross-module calls into `libsystem` from outside `core/sys/darwin/` split cleanly: **28 go through a `safe_` wrapper and 10 name the extern symbol directly.** The wrapped ones work — `core/io/process.vr` calls `safe_waitpid` and `safe_close` and compiles with **0 errors**, which is the positive control the class needed. The raw ones do not, where the symbol is declared inside the block: — core/shell/resources.vr   close, flock      8 errors, `unbound variable: …libsystem.close` core/sys/init.vr          exit x3           the abort path above core/mem/segment.vr       (mmap family)     6 errors, `unbound variable: mmap` core/mem/allocator.vr     errno x2          `errno` is declared OUTSIDE the block and resolves — So the rule is not "a raw extern name cannot be called" — it is "a name declared INSIDE `extern { }` cannot", and `errno` being raw and fine is what separates the two. `mem/segment.vr` and `mem/allocator.vr` are the allocator's page path, which makes this class more than a CLI concern. — **AND THE REAL BLAST RADIUS IS THE macOS PLATFORM LAYER: 13 FILES, 75 DIAGNOSTICS.** Both of our earlier counts came from grepping mount statements — mine found 10 raw call sites, verum-2b's found 12 instances in 3 files. Measured instead from the COMPILER'S diagnostics, matching every `unbound variable` against the 163 names declared inside an `extern { }` block anywhere in `core/`: — 20  core/sys/process_native.vr    process spawning 18  core/sys/darwin/thread.vr     threading 11  core/sys/darwin/time.vr       time 7  core/sys/darwin/tls.vr        thread-local storage 4  darwin/io.vr · mem/segment.vr · sys/fs_watch.vr 2  core/shell/resources.vr — most-missed names: 12 `close` · 7 `munmap` · 6 `kevent` · 5 `clock_gettime_ffi` · 4 `mach_absolute_time` — Verified independently here on `verum_v32`: thread.vr 18 unbound, process_native 20, time 11, tls 7 — matching name for name. So processes, threads, time, TLS, mmap and file-watching are all unreachable through these paths on macOS, with the abort path above sitting on top. The priority stays P1 rather than moving to P0 only because "18 diagnostics in thread.vr" and "threading does not work" are different claims and only the first is measured. — METHOD NOTE, and it is the sixth instance today: **where a full run exists, read the run — do not re-derive its subject from source text.** Both hand-greps understated this by an order of magnitude, and both looked thorough. — MINE UNDERSTATED IT FOR A REASON WORTH NAMING: the grep was `libsystem\.[a-z_]*(` **outside** `core/sys/darwin/`, which by construction cannot see the siblings INSIDE that directory mounting `super.libsystem`. Same directory is not the same module, so those are cross-module mounts too. The split on exactly that line: — INSIDE a platform dir (my grep excluded these)   5 files, 41 diagnostics 18 darwin/thread.vr · 11 darwin/time.vr · 7 darwin/tls.vr · 4 darwin/io.vr · 1 windows/winsock2.vr OUTSIDE                                          8 files, 34 diagnostics 20 sys/process_native.vr · 4 fs_watch · 4 mem/segment · 2 shell/resources · 1 each: term/raw/screen, sys/init, net/unix, mem/allocator — So the bounded repair list is **13 files / 75 diagnostics**, not the 8 I reported, and the after-number needs TWO greps: mine for the outside set plus `super.libsystem` for the siblings. An exclusion written to keep a grep clean removed half the population, and the grep still looked complete. | P1 | `core/sys/init.vr:623`, `core/sys/darwin/libsystem.vr:197` vs `:279`; T1085 | open — — healthy `let r = &x`      E312=1 -> 0 healthy `takes(&mk())`    E312=1 -> 0 GENUINE use-after-free    E312=1 -> 0 core/collections/list.vr  E312=4 -> 0 — **Fixing the false positive emptied the check, and that is the finding.** It reads as a regression and is not one: before, the analysis answered "dangling" to every question, which catches the true case the way a stopped clock is right twice a day. What it buys is that wiring the converter was IMPOSSIBLE (it would reject the standard library — four sites in `list.vr` alone) and is now merely USELESS. A blocker became a gap, pinned by `crates/verum_cbgr/tests/lifetime_successor_edge_tests.rs` in ci.yml's gating tier. — **An output that is entirely false positives looks exactly like a working detector until you ask it a question you know the answer to.** A true-positive column proves nothing alone; the healthy programs have to be in the same table. — WHAT REMAINS is the task's original substance and it needs new INPUT, not a better constraint. Rejecting `let r: &Int = { let v: Int = 5; &v };` requires knowing where `v`'s storage ends. The CFG carries DefSite/UseeSite for REFERENCES only, and `compute_liveness`'s backward propagation is an empty stub (`lifetime_analysis.rs:723-738`, body is two comment lines). `escape_analysis.rs` is the lead: it is block-aware, has a production consumer, and its `EscapeKind::ReturnEscape` names this shape — the question to measure first is whether it computes escape from the INNER BLOCK or only from the function. |
| A62 | **`.rev()` ON A RANGE GIVES THE LOOP VARIABLE `Item<T>`; `.rev()` ON AN ITERATOR DOES NOT.** NARROWED 2026-09-04 by the residue rather than by the failures: of 13 `core/` files using `.rev()`, ten carry an `Item<…>` diagnostic and three do not — and the three clean ones all write `xs.iter().rev()` while the ten write `(0..n).rev()`. Confirmed with the pair: `for x in buf.iter().rev()` is **0 errors**, `for i in (0..buf.len()).rev()` gives `expected 'Int', found 'Item<ISize>'`. So the adapter is not lossy — `Range::rev` is, and `Iterator::rev` is not. verum-2b's sweep sizes it independently at 30 diagnostics over 10 files, **29 of them the exact `Item<ISize>` shape**, consistent with `(0..len).rev()` where `len` is ISize. THE METHOD IS THE TRANSFERABLE PART: the three files that DID NOT break were the discriminator, and they were visible only because the count of users (13) and the count of breakers (10) were kept as different denominators. Sizing from failures alone would have given "13 files use it" and no predicate at all. — **COMPLETED: 13 of 13, no exceptions**, split by what `.rev()` is applied TO. `iter().rev()`: `base/iterator`, `math/nn`, `runtime/supervisor` — clean. `range.rev()`: `collections/slice`, `io/buffer`, `math/{elementary,linalg,rag,special,tensor}`, `mem/heap`, `net/dns`, `random/deterministic` — all break. `axes.iter().rev()` and `children.iter().rev()` are fine; `(0..buf.len()).rev()`, `(0..coeffs.len()).rev()` and `range_inclusive(count-1, 0).rev()` all lose the projection. **So `.rev()` is not what loses the `Item` — a RANGE's iterator is what does not survive it**, which moves the fix from the `Rev` adapter to `Range`'s iterator implementation and makes `(0..buf.len())` the load-bearing half of the seven-line repro rather than `.rev()`. Two corrections to the counting, both verum-2b's: `base/iterator.vr`'s occurrence is a DOC COMMENT, so the honest count of code users is **12, of which 10 break and 2 use `iter()`**; and their first split regex missed the parenthesised `(0..x).rev()` form, putting two breakers in a "neither" bucket — had that stood, the correlation would have read 8-of-13 with two unexplained, weak enough to dismiss as noise. **A residue is worth reading precisely when it is small enough to look like rounding.** — (Originally filed as:) **A `for` over `.rev()` gives the loop variable `Item<T>` instead of `T`.** Seven lines, with its own control — the same loop over the same range, one call apart: — `for i in 0..buf.len() { if buf[i] == 2 … }` compiles, **0 errors**; `for i in (0..buf.len()).rev() { … }` gives `error<E400>: Type mismatch: expected 'Int', found 'Item<ISize>'`. So the iterator's associated `Item` is projected for a bare range and left unprojected once `.rev()` is applied — the loop variable is typed as the projection rather than as what it projects to. — This is the associated-type face of A55 seen without any duplicate name: `Item` is not a colliding identifier, and the forward form proves the machinery works. — REACH: **13 files under `core/` use `.rev()`**, and the shape accounts for a visible share of the backlog's E400s — a spread sample of the known-failures list turns up `expected 'Item<[Int; 2]>', found 'Int'`, `'Item<[Int; 5]>'`, `'Item<[Int; 3]>'`, `expected 'USize', found 'Item<ISize>'` and `expected 'Int', found 'Item<ISize>'` among its most frequent pairs. Found by keying that census on the TYPE PAIR after a first attempt keyed on the message prefix reported "49 × Type mismatch" and said nothing. NARROWED TO THE PROJECTION RECEIVER 2026-09-06, with the compiler's own trace rather than by reading: `VERUM_TRACE_ASSOC=1 verum check` on the seven-line repro prints `[assoc-answer] ::Item of ISize -> <none>` three times, and the forward form prints NOTHING at all. So the question being asked is `Item` OF `ISize` — the range's ELEMENT type — and `<none>` is the correct answer to it; `ISize` has no `Item`. The receiver is already wrong before the projection is looked up, which is why the unreduced `Item<ISize>` reaches the loop variable. Neither `Rev<Range<ISize>>` nor `Range<ISize>` is ever asked. THREE FACTS THAT BOUND THE SEARCH: `core/base/iterator.vr` DOES carry `Iterator`, `DoubleEndedIterator`, `ExactSizeIterator` and `IntoIterator` for `Range<ISize>` (lines 3230-4594), so no impl is missing; binding the expression first (`let r = (0..n).rev(); for i in r`) fails IDENTICALLY, so it is not the `for` desugaring; and `xs.iter().rev()` is clean, so `Rev<I>`'s `type Item = I.Item` resolves when `I` is a normal iterator. AND THE DECISIVE ONE, an annotation rather than a loop: `let r: Rev<Range<Int>> = (0..10).rev();` reports `expected 'ISize', found 'Range<Int>'`. So `.rev()` ON A RANGE IS TYPED AS RETURNING THE ELEMENT TYPE, not `Rev<Self>` — and `let r = (0..10).rev();` with no annotation is 0 errors, because nothing then forces the answer to be examined. ONE MECHANISM EXPLAINS BOTH READINGS: the receiver is projected to its `Item` BEFORE the method is resolved, which is why the trace asks `::Item of ISize` (the element, already projected once) and why the result is `ISize`. `xs.iter().rev()` escapes it because an iterator's projection is not a range's. So the fix is not in `Rev`, not in `Range`'s impls — all four are present at `core/base/iterator.vr:3230-4594` — and not in the `for` desugaring, which a bound variable rules out. It is in whatever projects a method call's RECEIVER before resolution. | P2 | reproduction `for i in (0..n).rev()`, then `VERUM_TRACE_ASSOC=1` to see the receiver; `core/io/buffer.vr:623`, `core/async/stream.vr`, `core/math/rag.vr` | open |
| A63 | **`mount M.{CONST as Alias}` CANNOT RESOLVE A CONSTANT — the alias form works for types and functions and fails only for `const`.** Three lines with a three-way control, one property differing: `mount core.sys.linux.time.{CLOCK_MONOTONIC};` is **0 errors**; `mount core.sys.linux.time.{CLOCK_MONOTONIC as L_CM};` gives `error<E401>: cannot find `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` in module `core.sys.linux.time`` — naming a constant that IS declared there, at `core/sys/linux/time.vr:53`. Aliasing a TYPE (`{Maybe as M}`) and a FUNCTION (`{clock_gettime as cg}`) are both clean, so it is neither the `as` form nor that module. — POPULATION, anchored on `mount` lines: **3 files, all failing** — `core-tests/sys/{linux/mod,darwin/mod,mod}` — with 2, 6 and 2 `cannot find` diagnostics. An unanchored first count said 4 and 76: `X as Y` also matches a CAST (`AF_INET as UInt16` inside a record literal) and `mount … as …` matches the working type/function aliases. A run over 8 of those 76 reported zero, which is what sent me back to the key. — CORROBORATION IN-TREE: `core/database/postgres/async_listener.vr` carries a comment choosing a fully-qualified path over `mount connect as sync_connect` because "the rename-aliased import doesn't surface to AOT codegen" — a different symptom of the same alias handling, written by someone who worked around it rather than filing it. NARROWED BY READING 2026-09-05, three places ruled OUT so the next session does not re-walk them. (1) NOT the alias binding: `register_mount_item_alias` handles a TYPE via `lookup_type` and a VALUE via `ctx.env.lookup`, and a `const` is a value — and in any case the diagnostic fires BEFORE any alias is bound, so the two forms diverge at RESOLUTION, not at binding. (2) NOT ignorance of constants in general: the inline-module coverage gate explicitly matches `ItemKind::Const` alongside Function and Type. (3) NOT the inline-module path at all — `core.sys.linux.time` is a cross-FILE module, so the failing lookup is in the cross-file export registry. ANSWERED 2026-09-06 with the tree's own instruments, and the row's framing was wrong: the alias is not broken — it is the only spelling that EXPOSES an incomplete export table. `VERUM_TRACE_TASK21=1` prints the deciding gate for both forms, and they agree on the fact that matters: `mod='core.sys.linux.time' item='CLOCK_MONOTONIC' exports_len=21 export_hit=FALSE` in BOTH. The file declares FORTY public items (12 const, 20 fn, 8 type) and the probed table holds TWENTY-ONE. The two runs differ only in the early-return line that follows — plain: `bound_after=true`, aliased: `bound_after=false`. So the plain form is rescued because the name is already BOUND by an earlier path when the gate runs; the aliased form binds `L_CM`, never `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, so the gate checks the original name, finds it neither bound nor exported, and reports. AND THE KIND MATTERS FOR A SECOND REASON: aliasing a `fn` and a `type` from that same module both SUCCEED (measured: `clock_gettime as Aliased`, `LinuxTimeTimespec as Aliased`, 0 errors each) even though `export_hit` is false for them too — the trace shows functions taking a `[task21] resolved fd:` fallback that constants have no counterpart for. So there are two defects stacked: the export table is missing half the module's public surface, and the fallback that hides it covers functions but not constants. `exports.rs` DOES build a `Const` export (`ItemKind::Const` -> `ExportKind::Const` at ~836), so the loss is downstream of that. FOLLOWED DOWNSTREAM THE SAME SESSION, and the archive is NOT where it goes missing. `core.sys.linux.time` is a stdlib module, so its surface comes from the baked archive rather than from source — and the archive carries the constant: 33 occurrences of `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` in `stdlib_runtime.vbca`. Constants have a carrier there by design, as zero-arg FUNCTIONS flagged `is_const`, and `FunctionDescriptor::is_const`'s own doc names this very case as the reason the flag exists — "distinguishes `mount X.SOME_CONST` from `mount X.zero_arg_fn` and either rejects bare const references or silently mistypes plain zero-arg-fn references as values". `CoreMetadata` itself has fields for types, protocols, functions and implementations and none for constants, which is consistent: a constant is a function there. SO THE CHAIN IS COMPLETE and the remaining gap is one hop wide — the module's probed EXPORT TABLE is built from that metadata and comes out with 21 of the module's 40 public items, dropping the `is_const` functions. That is where to look, and it is a different place from both the alias path the row first named and the `exports.rs` source walk. MEASURED AGAIN 2026-09-06 WITH A COLLISION-FREE ALIAS NAME, and two of this row's own readings were artefacts of the probe. (a) Every aliased-const probe had used the alias name `K` — and `K` is a real stdlib constant, `core/hash/crypto/sha512.vr:83`, `const K: [UInt64; 80]` — so five different constants from three different modules all typed as `[UInt64]` and looked like one shared mis-binding. (b) The plain form is NOT a control. `CLOCK_TAI` resolves with NO `mount` LINE AT ALL — a file whose whole content is `fn main() -> Bool { return CLOCK_TAI; }` reports one type mismatch naming `Int` and no unbound diagnostic — so the plain form's 0 errors is not evidence that mounting a constant works; the name is already globally visible before any mount is read. The aliased spelling is the only one that exercises const resolution at all, which is why it is the only one that fails, and why `bound_after=true` on the plain run needs no mount to explain it.
