Correct EXACTLY for `T = Int`, the type every test of these APIs used. ROOT: two intercepts in `crates/verum_vbc/src/codegen/expressions.rs` — the Phase 0 site (~11550) takes T0622's witness channel (`LoadT{Generic}` + `CallM`), the second (~15157) emits `LoadI`. MEASURED: the second one does NOT need changing and must not be — a patch giving it the witness path was reverted, because one build showed all four acceptance checks passing WITHOUT it (`Result<Text>`, `Cell<Text>`, `Mutex<Text>` and the `List<Int>.iter().sum()` guard), while WITH it `sum` panicked `method 'ListIter.sum' not found on receiver of runtime kind Object`. The stdlib edits alone carry the repair, because the calls they contain reach Phase 0, which already had the channel. Its justification, *primitive T (the only T that can satisfy `where T: Default` AND have a known zero-default)*, asserts a closed set that is not closed: `Text` satisfies it. MEASURED BOUNDARY: rewriting to `T.default()` removes 18 pre-existing errors and introduces 6 — clean in RETURN position (whole-body `Err(_) => T.default()`, `Cell.new(T.default())`), E404-ambiguous in a match ARM, in an argument, and under a bare `let` annotation, which is exactly T0622's *binds T from the EXPECTED type*. RESIDUE LOCATED via a trace diff of the two-pole pair (`Maybe.unwrap_or_default` with `T.default()` = 2 errors, textually identical `Maybe.flatten` = 0). `VERUM_TRACE_AMBIG` prints the deciding line only for the sick half: `[ambig] bind=old resolved=None(Unit) or Some(&&_) free=["TypeVar(15606)"] fn_params=[]` — every TOLERATED ambiguity has a non-empty `fn_params`. The free var is NOT `T`: the exemption at `verum_types/src/infer/modules.rs` ~12534 already covers an impl block's params (T0741 consults `ctx.type_defs` for a var a TYPE NAME is bound to, precisely because a method of `implement<T> W<T>` declares no generics of its own), and a two-pole probe confirms it — in `implement<T> Box<T>`, `let old = *self` is exempt while an undetermined `List<_>` in the same block is still refused. What distinguishes the failing binding is the payload shape: the judge sees `Some(&&_)` — a DOUBLE reference with an undetermined inner type — where a healthy one sees `Some(_)`. NOT REPRODUCED MINIMALLY: a local `type Opt<T> is Nothing or Just(T)` with the identical `grab`/`or_def` pair gives `Just(_)` and is exempt, and so does an `implement<T> Maybe<T>` written in a user file against the real prelude `Maybe`. The extra reference level comes from `maybe.vr`'s own `Deref` impl (lines 44-87): `*Maybe<T>` is defined to evaluate to the INNER `T`, so `let old = *self` on `&mut Maybe<T>` goes through the user-Deref protocol rather than a plain deref — which is why no reconstruction reproduces it and why cutting the ORIGINAL down found it in one pass. The two-pole pair in that file is the smallest repro; the next step is whether the Deref path should apply to `&mut Self` at all. ACCEPTED 2026-09-04 on a freshly baked binary, twelve lines, none elided: `Result<Text>.unwrap_or_default()`, `Cell<Text>.take()`, `Cell`/`RefCell`/`RwLock`/`Mutex`/`Shared`.`default()`, `Shared.new_default()` and `Complex.from_real`/`from_imag` all carry the type's own default, and the Int controls (`unwrap_or_default`=0, `sum`=9, `sum_by`=9) do not move. Two lines LOOK wrong and the previous binary says they are not: `RefCell.default()` renders `[{, {0}}]` and `RwLock.default()` renders `[{{{1}, {0}, , {0}}}]` because `*guard` prints the whole struct — v32 rendered those same shapes with `0` where the empty Text now sits, so the payload changed and the brackets are a separate pre-existing rendering defect. Two earlier revisions of this row named a cause — first an exemption gap, then deref-of-`&mut`-variant — and a probe built to refute each did refute it; what is stated here is only what the traces show. Pins: `core-tests/base/maybe/regression_test.vr` T0622-LITERAL-IDENTITY-1, 11 across 5 modules. THE `Maybe.unwrap_or_default` HOLDOUT, MEASURED 2026-09-06 rather than described: replacing its `None => 0` with `None => T.default()` takes `core/base/maybe.vr` from 0 errors to 2, and BOTH are in OTHER functions — `take` and `replace`, which the row already noted call neither. The diagnostic is `error<E404>: Ambiguous type for 'old': the inferred type 'None(Unit) or Some(&&_)' is not fully determined`, on `let old = *self;` where `self: &mut Maybe<T>`. Two things in that string are the finding: the payload is `&&_`, a DOUBLE reference where `*self` on a `&mut Maybe<T>` should give `Maybe<T>`; and it is UNRESOLVED. So the literal is not a cosmetic workaround — it is what PINS a variable that nothing else pins, which is the persistent-registration-var mechanism `instantiate_variant_value` documents ("one `assert_eq(iter.next(), Maybe.None)` bound it to `Item<Range<Int>>` and every later `Maybe.Some(0)` in the FILE failed"). Binding it to `Int` is wrong and concrete; `T.default()` leaves it free and the deref in a different function has nothing to resolve against. That makes the holdout an INFERENCE defect, not a codegen one. REDUCED TO FOUR LINES the same day, outside the stdlib entirely — `type M<T> is None or Some(T);` with two methods in one `implement` block: a `public fn d(self) -> T where T: Default { T.default() }` and a `take(&mut self)` whose body is `let old = *self;`. Two errors. THREE DISCRIMINATORS, each one variable: ORDER decides — `T.default()` BEFORE `take` fails, `take` before `T.default()` is clean; the PROTOCOL decides — `T.zero()` and `T.one()` in the identical position are both clean, only `Default` does it; and the MATCH is irrelevant — a bare `T.default()` with no `match` and no `self` breaks it just as well, while a `match self` that never calls `default` does not. So the mechanism is: checking a method whose ONLY occurrence of `T` is the return type of `Default.default()` leaves a binding that a LATER `*self` in the same impl block resolves against, and `None => 0` was pinning it concretely. CORRECTING THIS ROW: it recorded source order as RULED OUT — measured here, order is decisive, so the earlier probe must have varied something else alongside it. A CANDIDATE MECHANISM, FOUND BY READING AND NOT CONFIRMED — labelled as such because the instrument built for exactly this class stays SILENT on the repro (see below): `crates/verum_types/src/protocol.rs:2613` registers `Default` as a BUILT-IN protocol whose `default` method has type `Type::function(List::new(), Type::Var(TypeVar::with_id(0)))` — no parameters, returning the SHARED Self var that all 69 built-in registrations use. Duplication is not the discriminator: `Eq`, `Clone`, `Hash`, `Ord` and `Deref` are each declared in `core/base/protocols.vr` AND registered built-in, and none of them leaks — because every one of their methods takes `self`, so the receiver binds Self at the call site. `default` is the ONLY built-in method that takes no parameters at all (measured over the whole file): `Self` occurs solely in its return type, and nothing at the call site freshens the shared var. `Zero` and `One` escape for a different reason again — they have no built-in registration, only the core declaration, so they resolve through the ordinary path. AND THE CODE ALREADY WARNS ABOUT THIS: `TypeVar::with_id` is documented "for testing", and `peek_counter` beside it names the hazard outright — "a `with_id`-minted id that the counter later crosses aliases an unrelated live var". So the fix belongs at the USE site, not the registration: resolving `T.default()` must substitute the Self placeholder with `T`, the way every receiver-taking method does implicitly. ONE CANDIDATE TESTED AND REFUTED 2026-09-06, recorded so the next session does not repeat it: `lookup_protocol_method_for_type_with_args` ALREADY inserts `subst_map.insert(Text::from("T0"), self_ty)` beside its `Self` mapping, with the comment "T0 mapping for TypeVar(0) which represents Self" — so the technique is established in the file. Adding the same insert to the three sibling lookups that key off `impl_.for_type` (`lookup_protocol_method`, `lookup_protocol_method_with_type_param_names`, `lookup_all_protocol_methods`) changed NOTHING measurable: the four-line repro still gives 2 errors, `core/base/maybe.vr` with `T.default()` still gives 3, and 152 `verum_types` suites stayed green. The change was reverted rather than committed — an edit with no measured effect is the mechanism-with-no-consumer this register keeps finding. So those three lookups are NOT the path this call takes. THE NEXT PROBE, stated so it is not re-derived: `T` here is a RIGID type parameter resolved through its `where T: Default` bound, not a concrete type with an impl — so the answer comes from the bound-discharge path (`ResolvedCallTarget::StaticCall` with `type_prefix_receiver`, `verum_types/src/infer/modules.rs` around 12736), not from `get_implementations`. Instrument that path first — print which lookup answers and what it hands back for `T.default()` — and only then decide where the placeholder should be substituted. `VERUM_TRACE_METHOD_LOOKUP=1` shows only the metadata registrations for this call and is not the instrument for it. THE DEFERRED PATH IS RULED OUT TOO: `VERUM_TRACE_CTOR=1` prints `proto-static discharge` ZERO times for the four-line repro, so `pending_protocol_static_calls` is for `Default.default()` (a PROTOCOL-name receiver), not for `T.default()` (a type-PARAMETER receiver). THE INSTRUMENT THAT DOES WORK is a differential trace — run both halves of the repro under `VERUM_TRACE_CTOR=1`, `sort` each, and `comm -23`: 2233 lines against 2230, and the seven that differ are the whole story. They are `method_call recv=T method=default`, a `skip_static_lookup=false precomputed=Some("Var(TypeVar(3479))")`, and two BINDS of freshly-minted vars to one older var (`bind v3479 := Var(TypeVar(3300))`, `bind v3480 := Var(TypeVar(3300))`). Two fresh vars collapsing onto the same older one is the shape to chase; the `bare_payload_ctor_as_fn` lines in that diff are noise — it returns `None` for both `T` (not a registered ctor) and `old`. AND THE `Var(0)` STORY IS NOT CONFIRMED BY THAT TRACE. `crates/verum_types/src/unify.rs:5253` carries a purpose-built diagnostic for precisely this class — "TEMPLATE-PIN TRAP (#41): binding a var that BELONGS to a registered type's declaration pins the TEMPLATE globally — always a bug upstream (a registry template leaked into unification unfreshened)" — and it fires ZERO times on both halves of the repro. The vars actually being bound are high-numbered fresh ones (3479, 3480 onto 3300), not a registry template. So the reading-based mechanism explains why `default` is SPECIAL among the built-ins, and it is a real inconsistency in `protocol.rs`, but it is not demonstrated to be THIS defect's cause. Whoever resumes should trust the differential trace over the reading. WHAT THE TRACE ACTUALLY SAYS, read line by line: the `Some(v)` arm binds `v` to `Var(3479)`, so the match's result type IS `Var(3479)`; the `None` arm's `T.default()` is then checked against it, and `bind v3479 := Var(TypeVar(3300))` says the call RETURNED a fresh, unconstrained var rather than `T`. A second `bind v3480 := Var(3300)` puts something else on the same var, and the `&&_` follows. So the defect is that `T.default()` does not tie its result to `T`. A CANDIDATE SITE IS LOCATED, `verum_types/src/infer/modules.rs:18838`: the bound-discharge arm calls `pc.get_method_type(bound_name, method_name)` and returns the answer VERBATIM — and `get_method_type` (`protocol.rs:7658`) hands back the protocol method's RETURN TYPE with no substitution of Self by the receiver. For every method that takes `self` the receiver later unifies against the first parameter and binds Self anyway; `default()` has no parameter, so nothing does. NOT VERIFIED, and the verification is one line: that arm sits under `if let Type::DynProtocol { bounds } = recv_ty`, and the trace shows `env.lookup("T")` answering `Unknown`, so whether a rigid bounded param even reaches it is unknown. DONE, AND THE CANDIDATE IS REFUTED: one `eprintln!` at 18838, one rebuild, and the bound-discharge arm fires ZERO times on the repro. It sits under `if let Type::DynProtocol { bounds } = recv_ty`, and a rigid bounded parameter does not reach it. Probing before editing is what made that one build instead of a build plus a revert. AND THE RETURNED VAR IS NOT FRESH: `infer_method_call_inner_impl` mints a fresh var in exactly ONE place (the `collect` short-circuit at 18518), and `Var(3300)` carries a LOWER id than the call's own vars (3479, 3480) — so it was minted earlier and is almost certainly `T`'s own inference var. Which means the two binds in the trace, `v3479 := Var(3300)` and `v3480 := Var(3300)`, are both CORRECT: the match arms tie to `T`. The leak is therefore NOT in what `T.default()` returns. SHARPENED TO ELEVEN LINES the same session, and the deref turned out to be a red herring: `T.default()` in one method plus `let r = self;` in the NEXT is enough, no `match`, no `*self`. The message then names `self`'s own type — `Ambiguous type for 'r': the inferred type '&mut None(Unit) or Some(_)' is not fully determined` — so it is `self`'s PAYLOAD that is unresolved in the second method. FOUR CONTROLS, one variable each: `let x = 1;` in place of `let r = self;` is clean; `T.zero()` in place of `T.default()` is clean; the second method alone is clean; and an explicit annotation (`let old: M<T> = *self;`) does NOT rescue it — which says the ambiguity is settled independently of what the author wrote down. That last control is the useful one: it rules out ordinary bidirectional inference. FULLY CHARACTERISED by four more controls the same session, and the answer is neither `self` nor the variant type — it is `T` ITSELF. `let r = self;`, `let r = None;`, `let r: M<T> = None;` and `let r = m;` for a PARAMETER `m: M<T>` all fail identically; a free function `g<T>(m: M<T>)` placed AFTER the impl block is clean, so the scope is the impl block. Then the two that settle it: `let r: M<Int> = None;` — a CONCRETE payload — is CLEAN, and `let r: List<T> = List.new();` — a different generic type that merely mentions `T` — FAILS with `Ambiguous type for r: List<_>`, i.e. `T` itself came back as `_`. That last control is a CLI measurement and cannot live in the unit harness: a bare `TypeChecker` has no stdlib, `List` does not resolve there, and the case reads clean for a reason that is not the language — the same trap that made an earlier revision of the A89 tests inert. And with no `let` at all (`fn f(&mut self) -> M<T> { None }`) it is clean. SO THE RULE IS: after a method in the block calls `T.default()`, any LATER `let` in that block whose type mentions the impl's parameter `T` is reported ambiguous. The impl's rigid `T` is consumed by the `Default` discharge and not restored for the next method. That is a statement about the impl block's type-parameter scope, not about deref, not about variants, and not about `unwrap_or_default` — the stdlib holdout is one instance of it. AND THE SCOPE IS EMPTY: `VERUM_TRACE_RIGID=1` prints `[rigid] scope for 'd': []` and `[rigid] scope for 'take': []` — for BOTH the failing and the clean variant. So the impl block's `T` is never entered as a RIGID parameter for its methods at all; it is an ordinary inference variable shared across the block, which is exactly the condition under which one method's use can reach another's. Whether that sharing is intended is the question for whoever resumes — if `T` were rigid per method, `T.default()` could not bind it and the later `let` would have nothing to inherit. The trace being IDENTICAL in both variants also means the empty scope is not itself the discriminator, only the precondition. AND THEN THE WHOLE IMPL-BLOCK FRAMING TURNED OUT TO BE AN ARTEFACT — corrected here rather than left standing. FOUR LINES, a FREE FUNCTION, no `implement` block, no variant type, no `self`: `fn d<T>() -> Int where T: Default { let x = T.default(); 1 }` gives `error<E404>: Ambiguous type for x`. Three controls, one variable each: `T.zero()` clean, `T.one()` clean, and `let x: T = T.default();` clean — an annotation pins it. Two more that isolate the trigger: the BOUND alone with no call is clean, and the CALL alone with no bound (`fn d(self) -> T { T.default() }`) is clean; it is the call RESOLVED THROUGH the bound that does it. SO THE STATEMENT IS SIMPLY: `Default.default()` does not tie its result to `T`, and any binding that does not otherwise pin it is ambiguous. Everything above — the impl block, the sibling method, the deref, `unwrap_or_default` — is downstream of that one fact; in `Maybe.unwrap_or_default` the `None` arm is pinned by the `Some(v) => v` arm, which is why the error surfaces in a sibling method instead of at the call, and why it took this long to see. THIS RE-SUPPORTS THE ORIGINAL READING by a different route: `default` is the only built-in protocol method with no parameter, so nothing binds Self — the support now comes from the four-line repro, not from the template-pin trap that stayed silent. AND THE TREE HAS ALREADY DIAGNOSED THIS EXACT FAILURE ONCE, for a different receiver shape. `infer/modules.rs` ~19325 carries T0741's comment verbatim: "`expect(self, msg: &Text) -> T` then returned a FRESH variable that nothing ever constrained, and the binding was reported as 'not fully determined'. Which is why `unwrap_or(d: T) -> T` was clean on the identical receiver: its argument re-links the two variables, so the missing step never showed. THE PARAMETER HAD TO APPEAR ONLY IN THE RETURN for the gap to be visible at all." That is A64's condition word for word. T0741's receiver was a `Type::Variant` whose nominal name the arm threw away, skipping the early-inherent path and its `[self-bind]` step; it was fixed by recovering the name from `variant_type_names`. A64's receiver is a `Type::Var` — the type parameter `T`, which the trace shows synthesising to `Var(3477)` — and it falls into the SAME `_ => None` arm, so the same `[self-bind]` step is skipped for the same reason. The fix therefore has a precedent in the same match: give the `Type::Var` receiver a key the way T0741 gave the `Type::Variant` one, so the bind step runs. NOT ATTEMPTED: a P1 inference change wants a full rebuild plus a `core/` blast-radius sweep against the previous binary, and this session had no cycle left for one — three of today's fixes needed exactly that sweep to catch a regression the suites missed. THE LAST CLAUSE — "the second intercept takes the witness path" — IS WITHDRAWN: the row's own measurement above shows a patch doing that was reverted because all four acceptance checks passed WITHOUT it and `sum` panicked WITH it. An acceptance written before the measurement should not outlive it. | open |
| A65 | **THE GRAMMAR ALLOWS IT, THE PARSER REFUSES IT, AND THE DOCS SHOW IT.** Two measured instances, both found by compiling what `internal:website docs/language/` actually prints, and both narrower than they look — in each the feature works and one spelling around it does not. (1) A quantified refinement in a TYPE's `where` clause: `type C is Int where value > 0;` parses, `fn f(..) where ensures (forall i in 0..3. xs[i] > 0)` parses, `type D is List<Int> where forall i in 0..3. self[i] > 0;` gives `E018: unexpected operator `>`, expected operator `=>``. `verum.ebnf` allows it (`value_where_clause = 'where', ['value'], refinement_expr`; a quantifier IS an expression), so grammar and docs agree and the parser is out of step. A float-literal theory (`-1.` eating the quantifier's `.`) was REFUTED — an identifier-terminated form fails identically. (2) A dotted declaration name with a capability: `type FileRead is File with [Read];` parses, `type File.Read is File with [Read];` gives `E044: expected `is` keyword`. Here `verum.ebnf` sides with the parser — `type_def` takes a plain `identifier` — so the DOC is the one out of step, in six places on `capability-types.md`. Both pages now carry a measured caution naming the working spelling. THIRD INSTANCE (2026-09-04): a parameterised pattern in CALL position. `pattern P(lo: Int)(x: Int) -> Bool = x > lo;` parses — the grammar's `pattern_type_params` allows the two-list declaration — and `let r = rx#"^a$";` parses, but `match e { P(rx#"^a$")(g) => .., _ => .. }` gives E018. NARROWED to one token by four probes: `P()(g)`, `P(1)(g)` and `P("^a$")(g)` all parse, `let r = rx#"^a$";` parses, and only `P(rx#"^a$")(g)` gives E018 — a tagged literal as a pattern ARGUMENT. The grammar's `active_pattern_tail` has `'(' expression_list ')' '(' [pattern_list] ')'` and a tagged literal is an expression, so this is the same grammar-versus-parser split as this row's first instance. `active-patterns.md` block 5 is the documented example. REPRODUCED on v32 (built 13:24, predating this session): all three give the same codes — E018, E018, E044. 2026-09-05, THE THREE INSTANCES TURNED OUT TO BE THREE DIFFERENT ROWS. Instance (1) is A84: a quantifier body beginning with `self` was read as field access because `is_quantifier_body_separator`'s "what starts a body" list omitted the `self` token — fixed there (T1177), and this row's own acceptance sentence is the probe. Instance (3) is A86: a tagged literal in an active pattern's argument list, whose cause is that the FIRST argument list is parsed as PATTERNS and converted with `pattern_to_expr` — the code says so in its own comment. Instance (2) is the only one where the grammar sides with the parser and the DOC is out of step, and it already carries the measured caution. Instance (2) is DONE 2026-09-06, and the census that closed it was wider than the page the row named: `capability-types.md` was already clean — its only remaining `File.Read` sits INSIDE the note that explains why the form does not exist, which is documentation of a real limitation rather than a paper-over. Three dotted DECLARATIONS survived elsewhere: two in `language/types.md` and one in `reference/grammar-ebnf.md`, the last of them on the page that prints the grammar the form contradicts. All three are now the prefix spelling (`DatabaseReadOnly`, `DatabaseFull`), and a re-count over `../website/docs` reports ZERO `type X.Y is` declarations, against a `core/` control that was already zero. Two apparent hits were NOT this defect and were left alone — `type U.Item: Display` in `syntax.md` and `generics.md` is an associated-type BOUND, a different production. The blocks stay gate-neutral: each carries a `{ ... }` body, and `check_doc_blocks_parse.py`'s `is_elision` classifies any block containing `...` as an elision, which is how 432 such placeholders coexist with the website's baseline of 0. | P2 | `type D is List<Int> where forall i in 0..3. self[i] > 0;` parses; the `capability-types.md` convention is either parseable or the page is rewritten to the plain spelling throughout, not left with a note | FIXED (docs) |
| A66 | **A SILENT IMPORT WAS HIDING A RING-LAW VIOLATION, AND REPAIRING THE IMPORT MADE IT VISIBLE.** `core/random/deterministic.vr` is a MOVED file whose relative imports came with it: `mount super.linalg` meant `core/math` when written and `core/random/linalg` — absent — after the move. A dotted path that resolves to nothing is silent (row A38), so the module compiled, mounted nothing, and read nil. T1143 replaced the three `super.` forms with absolute paths; `mount core.math.linalg.{Vector}` then became a REAL edge from ring 1.5 (byte-primitive) to ring 2 (data), which `make check-core-rings` correctly refuses. The violation is not new — the vector API always needed `Vector` — only its VISIBILITY is. Eight public functions carry it (`uniform_vec`, `uniform_range_vec`, `normal_vec`, `normal_vec_params`, `exponential_vec`, `categorical`, `categorical_logits`, `multinomial`) and all eight have ZERO callers, verified: the only name with hits outside the file was `categorical`, and all 57 were `categorical_sample` (math/tensor), `categorical_level` (msfs/strcat) or prose. Ring 2 is not `cohesive`, so a `math.*` sibling cannot depend on `math.linalg` either — but `math.distributed` and `math.stack_model` are already declared individually at ring 3, which is the established shape for exactly this. | P1 | `make check-core-rings` passes with no exception added to `core/rings.toml`; the eight functions live in a module declared above both rings; the scalar generators stay in `random/deterministic.vr`, which needs no `Vector`. | A67 | **`List<Int>.product()` RETURNS UNINITIALISED MEMORY.** Measured 2026-09-04 on two independent binaries, and the value differs run to run, which is the signature: `List.from([2,3,4]).product()` gave `-6888154834629107712`, `-4924178000321495040`, `88921726968635392` and `1167282220345671680` across four runs where 24 is the answer. OLD, across four dated binaries and twelve runs, with `sum` correct in every one: v32 (13:24) three runs three values; v37 (17:13) three runs three values; v39 (20:29) three runs three values; a build at 20:41 the same. v37 and v39 were run by another session on binaries this one does not have. A NEIGHBOURING and SEPARATE defect must not be confused with it: `List.iter().product()` and `List.iter().sum()` are CORRECT on v37 and PANIC on v39 (`method 'ListIter.sum' not found`), which is the codegen patch reverted in `1de77d482` and has nothing to do with this row — the two rows differ by one `.iter()`, behave oppositely, and a first attempt to date A67 measured the wrong one of the pair and concluded the opposite. `List.sum()` beside it is correct, and the two bodies differ only in the identity and the operator (`acc: T = 0` / `+` versus `acc: T = 1` / `*`), so the fault is narrow. A public stdlib API on the prelude's own container, returning garbage that typechecks. NARROWED by three probes that all PASS: `fn gen_mul<T: Mul + One>(a,b) -> T { a * b }` = 24; the same with `Add + Default` = 10; and `fn fold_mul<T: Mul + One>` over `&List<T>` using `xs[i]` = 24. So generic `Mul`, the `One` bound and a generic multiply-fold over a List are each fine. What is left is the ONE thing `product` does that the working `sum` beside it does not do differently: `acc = acc * *item` where `item` came from `unsafe { &*self.ptr.offset(i) }`. `sum` performs the identical deref and adds. Next step is one line — give `product` the `self[i]` form the passing probe used and see whether 24 comes back. BLAST RADIUS MEASURED: one method. `list.vr` has 81 sites using `unsafe { &*self.ptr.offset(i) }` and only `sum` and `product` combine it with an arithmetic accumulator; every other List operation checked on the same binary is correct — `len`=5, `l[0]`=3, `l[4]`=5, `contains`=true/false, `max`=Some(5), `min`=Some(1), `first`=Some(3), `last`=Some(5) — including `max`/`min`, which compare values read through that same pointer. VERIFIED 2026-09-04 22:38 on a binary baked at 22:33, six lines none elided: `product`=24, `sum`=9 (the control, unmoved), `float_product`=6.0, `empty_product`=1, and the T0622 guards (`result_default`=[], `iter_sum`=9) all hold. Twelve runs across four dated binaries gave twelve different garbage values before; every run since gives 24. FIX APPLIED: `product` indexes with `self[i]`; `sum` keeps the pointer form as the control. | P0 | `List.from([2,3,4]).product()` is 24 on three consecutive runs, and a non-Int element type gives its own product rather than a number | FIXED |
| A68 | **A THEOREM'S PARAMETER REACHES THE SOLVER AS `Int` WHATEVER ITS DECLARED TYPE, SO EVERY GOAL OVER A NON-`Int` PARAMETER IS UNPROVABLE.** Three theorems in one file, control first because `Int` is the goal side's default and therefore passes whether or not the declared type is consulted at all: `over_int(n: Int)` PROVES; `over_bool(b: Bool)` and `over_text(t: Text)` give E0319, and the hint names the cause — `over_bool (argument 0 is Int, declared "Bool")`. Refinement reflection declares the symbol with the parameter's real sort, the goal builds its application with `Int` for every argument, so the two sides describe different functions and Z3 refuses the application; the refusal is reported as an unproved goal, indistinguishable from a claim the solver could not show. **The fix is already in the tree, on the other entry point.** `verum verify` proves all three on the same binary, because `verify_cmd.rs:939` registers the value's SORT; `phases/proof_verification.rs:1489` registers only its type and says why, with a measurement: registering every parameter's sort made the corpus WORSE (23/68 specs and 275 unproved became 21/68 and 337). That measurement is not this fix — the two paths differ in WHAT COMPUTES the sort (`type_to_sort_and_name`, which answers an opaque `Verum!X` for any name it cannot resolve, versus `declared_sort_of`, which consults the record, variant and positional registries), so "an opaque sort is worse than Int" and "a resolved sort is better than either" are consistent and only the first was tried. Narrowed accordingly to `Int`, `Bool`, `Real` and `String` — exactly the sorts that cannot be an unresolved name. The record case (`Verum!CachedEntry`) stays open until those registries reach that entry point, **and it is filed as T1154** — a theorem parameter typed by a SIBLING module's type still reaches the solver as `Int`. **The prediction held exactly and that is the part to keep**: written BEFORE the build, the fix reaches a parameter whose type is declared in the theorem's own file and not one from a sibling module; measured after, `&Hash` and `&Version` discharged and `List<Version>` did not. The residue carries the name it was given in advance rather than one invented to fit the result. Found by the proving ground: both surviving obligations in the registry's cache module are this and nothing else. | P0 | `over_bool` and `over_text` discharge under `verum check`, AND the L3 proofs corpus is no worse than its re-taken baseline — a green probe alone is how the previous attempt got recorded as a regression | MET (T1151); record case → T1154 |

So the six are not one group. | P2 | `verum.ebnf` and CLAUDE.md list what the parser actually enforces, and the six contextual names are then either reserved deliberately or accepted as identifiers CLAUDE.md FIXED 2026-09-05: it now says the three are the CORE vocabulary and NOT the list the lexer refuses, names the fourteen, and points at `lexer_only_reserved`. The grammar already carried that distinction in a comment above the production. FIXED 2026-09-05, the remaining half: the production LINE now carries its own correction, so the grep hit says "CORE vocabulary ONLY — NOT the list the lexer refuses" and names `keyword` and `lexer_only_reserved`. A comment ABOVE a production is not what a reader who greps for it sees; the same line is. `check-grammar-docs-match` stays at 0 divergences across 364 documented productions. COST MEASURED THE SAME DAY, and it kept growing: FOUR documentation defects, each a name a writer reaches for BECAUSE the file said only three were taken — `for match in …` (cookbook/json.md), `let view: &NonEmpty<T>` (language/refinement-types.md), `meta fn rewrite_with(goal: &Goal, lemma: &Text)` (verification/tactic-dsl.md, `lemma` is in `proof_keywords`), and `@default(fn = Uuid.new_v4)` (language/meta/derives.md, an argument NAME). REPRODUCED on v32: same E018. POSITION MATRIX 2026-09-05: `type Path<T> is (T);` PARSES, and every use is refused — return type, parameter and `let` annotation all E018. The type can be declared and never mentioned, which is worse than an outright rejection because the definition looks accepted. A bare `Path(1)` call reaches type-checking (E412), consistent with the grammar claiming the name only where type arguments follow. THE NAME IS GENUINELY IN USE: `core/math/cubical.vr` writes `Path<A>(a, b)` in seventeen non-comment positions (parameter types, record fields) and the file parses, so the cubical production is not vestigial and cannot simply be gated away. | P2 | the diagnostic for `fn f(p: Path<Int>)` names the collision — it currently says 'unexpected token in expression: operator `<`' with no mention of cubical paths, endpoints, or that the name is reserved  FIXED 2026-09-05 (T1182) to exactly that acceptance — a DIAGNOSTIC, not a language change, because `core/math/cubical.vr` uses the production in seventeen positions and it cannot be gated away. `Path` followed by type arguments and NO `(` now says: "`Path` is reserved for cubical paths, which take two endpoints: `Path<A>(a, b)`", with the help line "if you meant an ordinary generic type, rename it — `RoutePath<T>`, `FsPath<T>`. The BARE name `Path` is free (that is `core.io.path.Path`); only `Path<…>` is claimed." Pinned by `crates/verum_fast_parser/tests/path_type_collision_tests.rs`, with two negative controls: a real `Path<A>(a, b)` still parses, and the bare `Path` is still an ordinary type. | FIXED |
| A71 | **`where requires` REQUIRES PARENTHESES; `where ensures` DOES NOT.** The two clauses have identical productions in `grammar/verum.ebnf` — keyword, expression, optionally more separated by commas — and behave differently. A first reading of 'the parser demands parentheses' was refuted by that single stdlib usage, which is unparenthesised and parses; the discriminator is the keyword, not the parentheses. REPRODUCED on v32: same E032. FULL MATRIX 2026-09-05, five keywords, two positions: bare `requires` and bare `ensures` parse; `where ensures` parses; `where requires`, `where invariant`, `where maintains`, bare `invariant` and bare `maintains` all give E032; `where decreases` gives E018. CORRECTION TO MY OWN COMMIT MESSAGE THAT DAY: it said `where requires` "does not exist". It does — with parentheses, exactly as this row measured. What is wrong is the ASYMMETRY: `where ensures x >= 0` parses bare and `where requires x >= 0` does not, from identical grammar productions. That half is untouched. | FIXED (docs) |

(3) `by def reversed` is a TWO-WORD justification; `proof_justification` is `by` plus a tactic or ONE identifier, and `def` is absent from the grammar — NOT fixed, the docs mean 'by the definition of' and no spelling exists. | A75 | **A `calc` CHAIN WRITTEN WITH TRAILING `by` ACCEPTS EXACTLY ONE STEP.** | P2 | Measured 2026-09-05 by step count, after five narrower hypotheses were each refuted in isolation: `calc { a = a by x; }` parses; adding a second identical step gives E018 `expected tactic expression`; three and four steps likewise. The OTHER documented spelling chains fine — `calc { a == { by x } a == { by y } a }` parses with two steps — so the two forms differ and nothing said so. `grammar/verum.ebnf` defines `calc_chain = 'calc' '{' expression calc_step { calc_step } '}'`, one or more, without distinguishing the spellings, so the grammar is the side that is right. CAUSE: `parse_tactic_seq` treats `;` as a TACTIC-SEQUENCE separator unless the token after it is on a stop list of proof-step and item keywords — and a calc relation (`=`, `==`, `<`, …) is on no such list. The tactic parser therefore consumed the step TERMINATOR and then demanded a tactic where the next relation stood, which is exactly what the diagnostic said and why five narrower hypotheses about `calc` missed it: the defect was in the tactic parser. FIXED 2026-09-05 (T1176): the chain marks the semicolon as belonging to the construct (`semicolon_ends_construct`), scoped to the step loop and restored on EVERY exit — the loop leaves through several `?`, and a leaked flag would quietly change how tactic sequences parse in whatever the caller did next. `website:docs/verification/proofs.md` uses four-step trailing-`by` chains, which is why that page kept failing after three rounds of narrower repairs. Pinned by `crates/verum_fast_parser/tests/calc_chain_steps_tests.rs`: one to four steps, the brace-justified spelling, and a negative control that a tactic sequence OUTSIDE a calc chain still spans semicolons. | FIXED |
