| A77 | **~~AN `ffi` BOUNDARY BLOCK ACCEPTS ONE OF THE GRAMMAR'S SIX ITEM FORMS~~ — WITHDRAWN 2026-09-05, THE ROW WAS WRONG AND ITS ERROR IS THE USEFUL PART.** | — | All six `ffi_item` forms parse. What the first measurement did was bisect them — each alternative ALONE inside `ffi F { ... }` — and every clause failed, so the row said five of six were unimplemented. A clause attaches to the function above it, so a block holding one clause and no function is the single arrangement in which they all fail. The isolation WAS the confound. Re-measured with a function first: `requires`, `ensures`, `memory_effects`, `thread_safe`, `errors_via` and `@ownership` all parse, `FFIFunction` carries a field for each (`crates/verum_ast/src/ffi.rs:111`), and the values are validated — `thread_safe = 42` gives "expected 'true' or 'false'", `memory_effects = Zzz` gives "expected memory effect", and an absurd line (`zzz nonsense here ;`) is still rejected, so nothing is being swallowed wholesale. What survives from the row: the cookbook page hung the clauses OUTSIDE the block as `@`-prefixed attributes, which is wrong under both authorities and is fixed; `core/` declares zero `ffi` blocks (5 files use `@extern("lib")` on free functions), which is an observation, not a defect. METHOD: when isolating one alternative changes the CONTEXT the alternative needs, the bisect measures the context. Keep the minimum that makes the construct well-formed, and carry an absurd-input control to prove the parser is still reading. | withdrawn |
| A78 | **A CAPABILITY ALTERNATION `with [Read \| Write]` IS IN THE GRAMMAR, DOCUMENTED AS A LANGUAGE FEATURE, AND REJECTED BY THE PARSER.** | P3 | `capability_item = capability_name \| capability_or_expr`, `capability_or_expr = capability_name , '\|' , capability_name , { '\|' , capability_name }` (`grammar/verum.ebnf` 1541-1548). Measured 2026-09-05 with the comma form as the control: `type Q is Text with [Read, Write];` parses, `with [Read \| Write];` gives `unclosed delimiter ']'`. Not a footnote: `website:docs/language/capability-types.md` carries a section *Composition via union* that teaches the form and distinguishes `[Read \| Execute]` (one slot, either suffices) from `[Read, Execute]` (two slots, both required). `parse_capability_list`'s own comment said "'\|' within a capability is handled in parse_capability_name" — `parse_capability_name` consumes one identifier and returns; that comment is corrected. SIZED BEFORE DEFERRING: `verum_ast::expr::Capability` has no union variant, 38 files reference `Capability::`, and "one slot satisfied by either" is a subtyping rule, not a parse — so this is a feature, not a parser patch. The documentation now states what compiles. | open |
| A79 | **A BASE-TYPE METHOD ON A REFINED TYPE IS A TIER DIVERGENCE: TIER 0 ANSWERS, TIER 1 ABORTS.** | P1 | Found 2026-09-05 by walking the getting-started tutorial end to end — its own test suite fails. Reproducer, ten lines, cut down from the tutorial rather than rebuilt: `type Greeting is Text { !self.is_empty() && self.len() < 100 };` / `fn greet(name: Text) -> Greeting { let msg = f"Hello, {name}!"; msg }` / `greet("Alice").contains("Alice")`. `verum diff-tiers` gives `tier0 exit 0, tier1 exit 134` — **verdict: DIVERGENT** — with `PANIC: AOT dispatch fault: no runtime candidate for method 'Greeting.contains'`. The message at `verum_codegen/src/llvm/instruction.rs:34873` names the class itself: "the receiver's static type was lost at compile time and its value carries no matchable type header (compiler defect: type-carry)". BOUNDED: `let g: Greeting = "hello"; g.contains(...)` does NOT reproduce, nor does plain `Text` — the value must come from a function whose declared return type is the refinement. `.len()` and `.is_empty()` on the same value are fine, so it is not every inherited method. NEAR NEIGHBOUR, NOT THE SAME: narrowing the reproducer further makes the panic name `Shared.new` instead, which is T1159 (P0, claimed) — a sign the two share the no-compiled-body mechanism, and a warning that a reproducer trimmed too far measures the other defect. WORKAROUND, MEASURED: annotate the binding with the base type — `let result: Text = greet("Alice")` — and both tiers exit 0. That is what the tutorial now does, with the reason stated. | open |
| A80 | **`where ensures` IS ACCEPTED ANYWHERE IN A FREE FUNCTION AND ONLY AS THE FIRST CLAUSE IN AN `implement` METHOD.** | P3 | Measured 2026-09-05 with the free function as the control, same clause text on both sides. THE ROW UNDERSTATED IT TWICE. First, the clause set diverged in FOUR ways, not one, and across THREE parsers, not two — `function_def` has `{ function_contract_clause }` (zero or more, any order, no separate production for a method) and each of the free function, the `implement` method and the protocol method implemented its own vocabulary. Parse errors, one clause set written in each position: `requires`+`ensures` 0/0/0; `requires`+`where ensures` 0/2/0; `decreases` 0/2/1; `@ghost ensures` 0/2/1; `contract#"…"` 0/2/0. Second, and worse: a method's `decreases` was PARSED AND THEN DROPPED (`decreases: Default::default()` at both the impl and protocol construction sites), so the termination measure never reached the AST — exactly the failure T1026's own comment in the free-function path warns about. FIXED 2026-09-05 in two halves. Parser (landed inside commit 013cb3084, a peer's T1170, which swept up the uncommitted work in the shared file): one door, `parse_function_contract_clauses`, called by all three, and `decreases` wired into the AST at all three sites; all six clause forms now agree. Checker (T1174): the measure reaching the AST changed nothing on its own, because TERMINATION CHECKING NEVER RAN ON A METHOD AT ALL — `find_recursive_calls_impl`'s `MethodCall` arm recorded nothing, so the call list was empty for every method and each per-call check was unreachable, and the `is_recursive` gate asked a SECOND, narrower walk covering six expression kinds against the real walker's seventy-three. Measured before the fix: a method with no base case and no measure, and a method whose declared `decreases` provably INCREASES, both type-checked CLEAN where the identical free function is refused `error<E321>`. THREE walks answered one question and disagreed: the `is_recursive` gate's narrow walk (six expression kinds), `find_recursive_calls_impl` (seventy-three, but blind to a self method call), and `check_block_termination`, which hand-walked exactly two STATEMENT kinds — so a `return self.m(n+1);` was found by the gate and then never checked. All three go through one walk now, and the narrow one is deleted — but NOT its distinction: the narrow gate's comment said that inside an `implement` block a bare `name(…)` is the imported FREE function, and that is right. `core/base/primitives.vr` writes `public fn signum(self) -> Int { signum(self) }`, forwarding to the intrinsic. Losing the distinction took that file from 2 errors to 189 — while 120 parser suites, 151 `verum_types` suites, 44 shipped example programs and all thirteen targeted probes stayed GREEN. It was caught by running one sample of `core/` through a worktree build of the PREVIOUS commit and diffing the verdicts, which is the only instrument that touched the corpus this change actually alters. `RecursiveCall` now records whether the call was written `self.name(…)`, and in a method only those count. A SECOND core site changed and is a TRUE positive, not a regression: `core/collections/list.vr`'s `introsort_loop` recurses on `self` inside a `while`, and the identical shape written as a FREE function is refused by the same rule on BOTH sides of this change — measured — so the checker was not being made stricter for methods than for anyone else, it was reaching a method for the first time. It terminates for two reasons a syntactic walk cannot see (each call takes a strictly smaller partition; `depth` counts down to the heapsort fallback), so it carries `@allow(unbounded_recursion)` with both reasons stated — the attribute `core/` already uses at five other sites. FULL SWEEP 2026-09-06 over all 855 `core/` files that contain both an `implement` block and a `self.` call — the only ones this change can reach: ELEVEN report E321, and SIX of them reported it before the change too. Of the five new ones — `math/agent.vr`, `math/guardrails.vr`, `signal.vr`, `sys/linux/io.vr`, `term/raw/termios.vr` — all five are one shape (`collections/list.vr`, the true positive above, was already annotated by the time this sweep ran and so does not appear): a PROTOCOL impl whose method forwards to the INHERENT method of the same name — `implement … ExecutableTool for CallableTool { fn schema(&self) … { self.schema() } }`, where the inner call is `CallableTool`'s own. A name-based walk cannot tell that from recursion, so the check now claims only the INHERENT case, where `self.name(…)` unambiguously means this method. That is a stated limit, not an oversight: recursion inside a protocol impl goes unchecked, and saying so is better than a diagnostic that is wrong four times out of five. Pinned by `crates/verum_types/tests/method_termination_tests.rs` and `crates/verum_fast_parser/tests/context_clause_position_tests.rs`. | FIXED |
| A81 | **EVERY DOCUMENTATION GATE IS INERT IN CI: THE TREE THEY MEASURE IS NOT IN THE CHECKOUT.** | P2 | Measured 2026-09-05. Six gates read the website documentation through a gitignored local symlink — `check_doc_examples`, `check_doc_blocks_parse`, `check_doc_anchors`, `check_doc_error_codes_exist`, `check_grammar_docs_match`, `check_homepage_examples`. The website is a separate repository (`verum-lang/website`, public), the symlink's parent directory is in `.gitignore`, and `git ls-files` over it returns **0**: in a CI checkout the path did not exist, so the gates CI ran on documentation measured nothing. Four of the six said so out loud ("SKIPPED — NOT CHECKED"), which is the honest shape; `check_doc_examples` printed `0 example(s) a reader cannot trust` and returned 0 — a zero indistinguishable from a clean estate, and it is the one wired into `strict-gates` as a ratchet. FIXED 2026-09-05, in three parts. (1) The empty run reports itself instead of passing, and four gates read `REPO.parent/website/docs` or `VERUM_DOCS_DIR` rather than the symlink. (2) `strict-gates` now CHECKS THE WEBSITE OUT (`actions/checkout` → `.website`, `continue-on-error` so a website outage cannot block a compiler PR) and sets `VERUM_DOCS_DIR=.website/docs` for the gate step — all six of the named gates plus `check_doc_indented_blocks` and `check_doc_cli_flags` run against a real tree. (3) `check_doc_blocks_parse`, which had no CI caller at all and no `--check` flag on its one invocation, gates FOUR trees against per-tree baselines (T1171, A82). Measured after the change: `check-doc-anchors` sees 605 anchor links and `check-grammar-docs-match` 364 constructs, both of which were zero to CI. | FIXED |
| A82 | **NO GATE COVERS `docs/architecture/`, AND 73 OF ITS 131 VERUM BLOCKS DO NOT PARSE.** | P3 | Measured 2026-09-05 across all 41 files with the website census's own classifier. THE GATE EXISTED AND MEASURED NOTHING: `check_doc_blocks_parse` had been a Makefile target for weeks with no CI caller, and the one invocation it had omitted `--check`, so it could not fail a build; its single global BASELINE could only ever describe the tree it was counted on. FIXED 2026-09-05 (T1171): per-tree baselines (`TREE_BASELINES`), `--check` wired into CI's `strict-gates` over FOUR trees, and a tree nobody has counted now FAILS `--check` rather than passing on a borrowed number — proved in both polarities by the script's own `--self-test`. THE TWO SMALL TREES ARE AT ZERO, worked down rather than baselined: `vcs/` 12 -> 0 and `core-tests/` 3 -> 0, every fix measured per block and applied only where the PARSE-error count strictly fell. What they were: Rust leaking into a ```verum fence (`#[repr(C)]`, `#[derive(…)]`, `struct`, `quote!`, `assert!`, `::<T>` turbofish, `fn main() async`), an API named that does not exist (`Heap.alloc_zeroed`, `channel::<Int>.bounded`, `Json.parse` — the real ones are `Heap.new`, `core.async.channel.bounded`, `core.encoding.json.parse`), a named-tuple field carrying an attribute where the grammar's `tuple_type` is positional, and `{ ... }` as an elision — which parses NOWHERE, while the language has `todo()` and an empty record `{ }` for exactly that. THREE BLOCKS WERE GENUINELY ASPIRATIONAL and now say so in a comment (the census has a counter-example class for that): type-level arithmetic in a return type and `@quote` interpolation, both pinned as REFUSED by the suite's own `_fail` tests beside them. TWO FENCES WERE MISLABELLED — pi-calculus notation and a bare `match` arm are not Verum, and the tag said they were. `docs/` STAYS AT 62, all of them inside `msfs-diakrisis-machine-verification.md` (47) and `verum-verification-architecture.md` (15), design documents proposing syntax the language does not have: the grammar defines `∀`/`∈` only as GLOSSES inside `(* comments *)` for `forall`/`in`, and has no spelling at all for `⊨ ⟹ ∪ ∉ ⊋ ∞ :=`. Rewriting those is authoring a design, not fixing documentation; the gate holds them at 62 so the population cannot grow. | FIXED (gate) |
| A83 | **THE 1.4 M LOC/sec PARSE FIGURE DOES NOT REPRODUCE: A RELEASE BENCH GIVES 370–490 K.** | P3 | `crates/verum_compiler/tests/compilation_speed_contract.rs:42` carries `Actual measured on main (2026-04) on macOS arm64: ~1.4 M LOC/sec`, CLAUDE.md repeats it, and the marketing homepage printed it as a headline number. Re-measured 2026-09-05 with `cargo bench -p verum_fast_parser --bench production_targets`, the repository's own bench on the same generator the contract test uses: parse 820 LOC **448 K/s**, 4100 LOC **489 K/s**, 8200 LOC **370 K/s**; lex 10 K LOC **2.48 M/s**. The debug contract test the same day: parse 226 K/s, lex 920 K/s. Three-ish times below the cited figure at every size. CAVEAT STATED RATHER THAN HIDDEN: the machine carried a load average of 20–80 from another session throughout, so these are a FLOOR, not a clean number — but the criterion intervals are tight (1.70–1.97 ms on the 820-LOC case), so noise alone does not span a 3x gap. The lex figure is close to the cited one, which is the shape of a lex/parse conflation and the first thing to check. The gate itself is unaffected: `PARSE_FLOOR_LOC_PER_SEC = 50_000` passes with room. The homepage now prints the lex figure, names the bench, and gives the parse range. SECOND FIGURE, SAME SHAPE: CLAUDE.md and the homepage also carried `CBGR check … measured ~0.93ns — production_targets bench`. That bench name is right — there are TWO benches called `production_targets`, one in `verum_fast_parser` and one in `verum_cbgr` — and running the CBGR one on 2026-09-05 gives `generation_check_valid` **1.61 ns**, `generation_and_epoch_check` **1.69 ns**, `generation_check_invalid` **1.20 ns**, and `100_checks_batch` **125.45 ns**, i.e. 1.25 ns per check. Between 1.3x and 1.8x the cited figure, on a loaded machine, so this one is much closer than the parse case and could well be load alone. Either way the 15 ns design budget holds with an order of magnitude to spare. Homepage updated to the measured range with the bench named. THE PATTERN, WHICH IS THE POINT OF THIS ROW: three headline numbers, all sourced from comments dated months back, none reproducible by running the thing the comment names. A number in a comment is not a measurement — it is the memory of one. FIXED 2026-09-05: all three sources now carry the measured ranges WITH the date and the bench that produced them — the homepage (done earlier that day), CLAUDE.md's Performance Targets block, and the contract test's own comment, which was the origin the other two copied. Each now also states the load caveat rather than hiding it, and says plainly that the figures are floors. The gate is untouched: it checks the 50 K floor, not the measured figure. | FIXED |
| A84 | **`self[i]` INSIDE A QUANTIFIER BODY DOES NOT PARSE; `(self)[i]` DOES.** | P2 | Measured 2026-09-05 with three controls that each isolate one variable. FAILED: `type S is List<Int> where forall i in 0..10. self[i] == 1;` — `unexpected operator '=='`, and the same for `<`, `<=` and any operator after the index. PARSED: `type S is List<Int> { self[0] == 1 };` (indexing `self` in a refinement with no quantifier), `type S is List<Int> { self.len() > 0 };` (`self` with a call), and `fn f(xs: List<Int>) -> Bool requires forall i in 0..10. xs[i] == 1 { true }` (indexing a PARAMETER inside a quantifier body). So it is neither `self`, nor indexing, nor quantifiers — it is `self` indexed INSIDE a quantifier body; the braced form failed identically. CAUSE: after a quantifier's domain, `is_quantifier_body_separator` decides whether the `.` starts the BODY or is member access by looking at the token AFTER it, and its list covered identifiers, `(`, `[`, `!`, nested quantifiers, booleans and numbers — everything else fell to "not a separator". `self` is its own token, so `. self[i] == 1` was read as field access `.self`, then `[i]` as postfix, and the domain parser met `==` below its minimum precedence and stopped, leaving the caller to expect `.` or `=>`. `(self)[i]` worked because `.(` was already on the list — the parenthesis is what named the cause. FIXED 2026-09-05 (T1177) by the criterion the list was reaching for: a token that CANNOT be a field name starts a body — `self`, `Self`, a prefix operator (`-`, `*`, `&`, `~`), a string or char literal. Documentation consequence: `website:docs/language/quantifiers.md` states quantifiers are usable "anywhere an expression is expected" and its sorted-list example is the natural spelling of exactly this. Pinned by `crates/verum_fast_parser/tests/quantifier_body_start_tests.rs`, including a negative control that a method chain in the domain is still member access. | FIXED |
| A85 | **A THEOREM WITH `-> T` AND NO STATEMENT IS REFUSED; WITHOUT THE RETURN TYPE IT PARSES.** | P3 | `theorem_tail` (`grammar/verum.ebnf`) makes `[ '->' , type_expr ]` optional and INDEPENDENT of the statement, so a theorem may carry a return type, no contract clause, no `:` expression, and a proof body. Measured 2026-09-05: `theorem t() { proof by simp }` parses — with no statement at all — and so do `theorem t(): true { … }` and `theorem t() ensures true { … }`. `theorem t() -> Int { proof by simp }` gave `unexpected keyword 'by'`. Adding a statement rescued it: `theorem t() -> Int: true { … }` parses. So it was the return type WITHOUT a statement, and the grammar is the side that is right. CAUSE: the theorem parser read its return type with `parse_type_no_sigma`, which is not a SIGNATURE context — so the proof body's `{ … }` was taken for a REFINEMENT PREDICATE on the return type, and the `by` inside it was the reported error. That also explains why a statement rescued it: the brace then no longer followed the type. FIXED 2026-09-05 (T1180) with `parse_theorem_return_type`, which is the signature context with sigma still disabled (a theorem's `:` introduces the PROPOSITION). THE FIRST ATTEMPT WAS INERT and the test said so: `signature_ctx` lives BEHIND `use_lookahead`, and the new helper had copied `parse_type_no_sigma`'s `use_lookahead = false` — so the branch never ran and exactly one of the four combinations still failed, the return-type-only one. Three green and one red is what an inert flag looks like when the other three were already passing. `brace_group_is_refinement` already decides this correctly for functions — a group is a refinement exactly when the signature CONTINUES after it, and after a proof body nothing does — so no new heuristic was invented. `parse_axiom` keeps `parse_type_no_sigma`: an axiom has no body, so the question does not arise there. One arm had to be ADDED to `brace_group_is_refinement` for this: a `:` after the group means a theorem's statement follows, so the signature continues and the group WAS a refinement — without it, giving the theorem a signature context would have turned a real refinement (`-> Int{> 0}: true { … }`) into a body. A function signature has no `:` in that position, so the arm cannot fire there. The fix's own negative control is what asked the question. Documentation consequence, already fixed: `website:docs/reference/tactics.md` wrote `theorem refl<T>(x: T) -> Path<T>(x, x)` — the Curry-Howard reading where the return type IS the claim. Pinned by `crates/verum_fast_parser/tests/theorem_return_type_tests.rs`, all four combinations plus a negative control that a genuine refinement on a theorem's return type still parses. | FIXED |
| A86 | **A TAGGED LITERAL IS REJECTED IN AN ACTIVE-PATTERN ARGUMENT, WHERE A PLAIN STRING IS ACCEPTED.** | P3 | Measured 2026-09-05 by substituting one token. FAILS: `match e { M(rx#"^a$")(g) => …, _ => … }` — `expected pattern`. PARSES: the same line with `"^a$"` instead of `rx#"^a$"`. Each piece is fine on its own: `let r = rx#"^a$";` parses as an expression, `M(arg)(bind) => …` parses as an active-pattern arm, and `P(bind) => …` parses. It is a TAGGED literal in the argument position of a parameterised pattern that the pattern parser will not take, which is where the natural spelling of a regex-matching active pattern lives. CAUSE FOUND BY READING 2026-09-05, and the code says it outright: an active pattern's FIRST argument list is parsed as PATTERNS and then converted with `pattern_to_expr` — the comment there reads "this is a simplification - we parse as patterns but need expressions" and lists the three ways out. A tagged literal is an expression form with no pattern spelling, so the pattern parser refuses it before the conversion is ever reached. SIZED 2026-09-05, and the sizing corrected twice by reading. (a) A BACKTRACK CANNOT WORK: `pos_before_args` is already saved at the parse site and never used, but the list that fails to parse as patterns never REACHES the `if second (` check — the error is returned first. The discriminator has to run BEFORE the list is parsed, and it is available: the token after the balanced group (`peek_after_balanced_parens`, currently private to `expr.rs`). (b) THE GRAMMAR IS NOT ON THE DOCUMENTED SPELLING'S SIDE for patterns: `literal_pattern` admits an optional `-` on the numeric forms, then integer, float, string, char, byte or bool — and nothing else; deliberately EXCLUDES tagged literals and its comment says so, so widening the pattern parser would be wrong. What the grammar does say is `active_pattern_tail = '(' expression_list ')' '(' [pattern_list] ')'` — the first list is an EXPRESSION list, and parsing it as patterns is the admitted simplification. So the fix is: on seeing a second `(` ahead, parse the first list with the EXPRESSION parser. That needs a comma-separated expression-argument parser in `pattern.rs` and the active-pattern construction extracted so both paths can reach it — FIXED 2026-09-06, and the shape is what the row predicted: a discriminator that runs BEFORE the list is parsed. `active_pattern_first_list_is_expressions` counts parens from the already-consumed `(` and reports whether a `(` follows the matching `)`; it only counts, so it cannot itself fail on a form the pattern parser refuses. On `true` the list goes to `comma_separated(\|p\| p.parse_expr_bp(0))` — the grammar says `expression_list`, and `parse_call_arg` was the wrong primitive twice over: it is private to the `expr` module (the call would not have compiled) and it admits a named-argument form this production does not have. The dead `pos_before_args`, saved for a backtrack this row proved cannot work, is gone. MEASURED with a four-case control set in `active_pattern_argument_list_tests.rs`, and the control is HONEST-RED: on the pre-fix `pattern.rs` exactly one case fails — the tagged literal — and the other three pass, so the test discriminates rather than merely agreeing. The three guards are the other spellings of the same first list: a plain literal, the empty `Even()(n)` (handled by a branch ABOVE the discriminator), and a plain variant `A(x)` (no second paren group, so its list stays patterns). `cargo test -p verum_fast_parser --tests` is 120 suites, 0 failures. BLAST RADIUS IS NIL BY CONSTRUCTION and was measured rather than assumed: zero files in `core/` and zero in `core-tests/` use `Name(args)(binds)` in a match arm, so the changed path is never entered there. Admitting a tagged literal into patterns generally would be wrong; it is not a pattern. Documentation consequence: `website:docs/language/active-patterns.md`'s worked example is exactly this shape. | FIXED |
| A87 | **THE DOCUMENTATION'S SYNTAX DEFECTS ARE A SMALL SET OF CLASSES, AND THE CLASS LIST IS THE USEFUL ARTEFACT.** | P3 | 2026-09-05: 181 measured defects across the website, ~120 closed by hand in one session. They were not 181 different mistakes. GRAMMAR-KNOWS-BEST (the docs invented a form): `where requires` needs parentheses while `where ensures` does not; `throws(E)` sits BEFORE `->`; `linear` qualifies a TYPE, not a parameter; contexts do not sum with `+`; a loop is `for await`, not `async for`; `@implements(T, Debug)` takes parentheses, not `<>`; `@cfg` is an ATTRIBUTE, never a type expression; `protected` is not a protocol-item visibility; `context protocol X { }` takes no closing `;`; a const expression in a type argument is bare, not braced; `pattern P(…) -> T` needs `=`; a comment is not a `const` value; `sh#"…"` has no closing `#`; `macro name { }` does not exist; `quote(N)` is an expression, never a type; `@quote(...)` does not exist and `quote { … }` does. RESERVED WORDS AS IDENTIFIERS: `match`, `view`, `lemma` (twice), `fn` — four defects from one sentence in CLAUDE.md (A69). NOT-CODE-AT-ALL: ~40 blocks were API catalogues, tactic notation, or grammar illustrations fenced as ```verum. MISSING TERMINATOR: ~25, mechanical. PARSER GAPS FOUND ALONG THE WAY, each its own row: A78, A79 (withdrawn), A80, A84, A85, A86. THE LESSON, WHICH IS WHY THIS ROW EXISTS: diagnose by grouping the parser's own message across a directory before fixing anything — the top three messages covered two thirds of every file measured. CLOSED OUT 2026-09-05: a full recount gives **17 DEFECT of 2795 blocks**, down from 213 of 2845. The estate shrank by 48 blocks because that many were catalogues that became tables. The 17 are sixteen files with one defect each — the classes are gone and the residue is individual, which is exactly the shape a ratchet is for. `check_doc_blocks_parse.py`'s BASELINE is 17. | FIXED (docs) |
| A88 | **A CONTEXT CLAUSE BEFORE THE RETURN TYPE PARSES IN A FREE FUNCTION AND IS REFUSED IN AN `implement` METHOD — THE SECOND INSTANCE OF EXACTLY THIS ASYMMETRY.** | P3 | Measured 2026-09-05 with the free function as the control, identical text on both sides. FREE: `fn new() using [Database] -> U { … }` parses. IMPL: the same signature inside `implement U { … }` gave `error<E056>: expected '{' or ';' after impl method signature` — a diagnostic naming the brace, not the clause that stopped it. THERE WERE THREE PARSERS, NOT TWO: the protocol-method signature parser had the same omission and a different diagnostic (`error<E018>: expected protocol item`), found only because the fix went looking for other copies. Six signatures, identical but for where they live: free/pre ok, free/post ok, impl/pre E056, impl/post ok, protocol/pre E018, protocol/post ok. FIXED 2026-09-05 (T1169): one door, `parse_optional_context_clause`, called once before `->` and once after by all three — the second call is a no-op when the first consumed a clause, which is also the grammar's "at most one". AFTER: all six parse, 116 `verum_fast_parser` suites green, 44 shipped example programs green, `crates/verum_cli/examples/async_context.vr` (where the row was found) clean, and `vcs/specs/L2-standard/contexts/README.md` block 2 — blocked by this defect — now parses. Pinned by `crates/verum_fast_parser/tests/context_clause_position_tests.rs`, including a negative control that two clauses on one signature stay refused; fix-sensitivity measured by removing the two added calls (exactly the impl and protocol tests fail). A80 IS THE SAME SHAPE ONE CLAUSE OVER (`where ensures` accepted anywhere in a free function, only first in an impl method) and is NOT fixed by this change. | FIXED |
| A89 | **INDEXING THROUGH A REFERENCE TO A TYPE ALIAS FAILS; BY VALUE AND WITHOUT THE ALIAS BOTH WORK.** | P2 | Measured 2026-09-05 with two controls, one variable changed at a time. FAILED: `type M is List<List<Float>>; fn f(m: &M) -> Float { m[0][0] }` — `Cannot index non-indexable type`, and the same for `&checked M` and `&unsafe M`. PASSED: the same body with `m: M` (by value), and `fn f(m: &List<List<Float>>)` (the reference WITHOUT the alias). So it is neither references nor aliases nor indexing — it is a reference whose pointee is a NAMED type the Index-protocol lookup cannot resolve. CAUSE: the lenient fallback ("a named type may implement Index; give it a fresh element type") matched on the OUTER type, and `Type::Reference{…}` matches none of its arms — so every reference whose pointee needed that leniency fell through to the error, while the identical pointee written by value took the lenient arm. FIXED 2026-09-05: the fallback decides on the POINTEE (`inner_ty.unwrap_or(&arr_result.ty)`), which is what the reference tiers already peel for the protocol lookup a few lines above; the diagnostic still names the type the user wrote. `crates/verum_cli/examples/cbgr_references.vr` — the shipped example whose subject is the three reference tiers — DECLARED `type Matrix is List<List<Float>>;` and then used it NOWHERE: all five signatures spelled the expansion out, which was the workaround. They use the alias again. Pinned by `crates/verum_types/tests/index_through_reference_tests.rs` — the AGREEMENT between by-value and by-reference at all three tiers, plus a negative control that `&Int` stays refused. A first revision of that file asserted ACCEPTANCE by value and failed for a reason that is not the language: the harness builds a bare `TypeChecker` with no stdlib, so `type M is List<List<Float>>` registers M as `<placeholder:M>` — neither Named nor Generic nor Unknown nor Var — and the lenient arm does not fire for the by-value form either. Through the CLI, where `List` resolves, both forms give 0 errors. The absolute claim belongs to a CLI probe; the agreement is what a stdlib-less harness can honestly pin. | FIXED |
| A90 | **A `&checked mut` BORROW IS NEVER RELEASED: READING THE VALUE AFTER THE CALL IS REFUSED, WHERE `&mut` IS FINE.** | P1 | Measured 2026-09-05, one token changed between the two probes. FAILED: `fn s(x: &checked mut List<Int>) { }` then `let mut n = List.from([1,2]); s(&checked mut n); print(f"{n:?}");` — `error<E310>: cannot borrow 'n' as immutable because it is also borrowed as mutable`. PASSED: the identical program with `&mut`. ROOT CAUSE, WIDER THAN THE ROW: `infer_unop`'s four reference arms each carried their OWN copy of the borrow-tracking rule — 90, 60, 9 and 13 lines — and the two tier-1 copies had drifted. Writing each of five borrow shapes twice, once per tier, FOUR diverged: `&checked x` and `&checked mut x` through a call stayed borrowed (tier 1 stricter); `&checked mut s.a` and `&checked mut xs[0]` registered NOTHING, so tier 1 ACCEPTED a whole-value `&mut s` that tier 0 refuses (tier 1 UNSOUND). Two of the four point each way. FIXED 2026-09-05: one carrier, `track_reference_borrow`, called by all four arms (-169/+148 lines); `&checked` is `&` with the RUNTIME check elided, so its STATIC verdict must be identical. AFTER: all five shapes agree, 53 aliasing tests green, 44 shipped example programs green, and `crates/verum_cli/examples/cbgr_references.vr` — the shipped example whose subject is exactly this — reads its value after the call again instead of carrying a workaround comment. Pinned by `crates/verum_types/tests/reference_tier_parity_tests.rs` (CI `integration`). ITS FIX-SENSITIVITY WAS MEASURED, and two earlier revisions of that file were INERT — samples using `List`/`print` reported `TypeNotFound` and E100 in a harness with no stdlib while the assertion compared `is_empty()`, and a read-back written `let r = n;` is a USE, not a borrow, so it tripped nothing at either tier, negative control included. | FIXED |
| A91 | **A REFINEMENT REACHES A THEOREM BUT NOT ITS ARITHMETIC: `n > 0` IS PROVED, `n * 2 > 0` IS NOT.** | P2 | Measured 2026-09-05, narrowing one step at a time. PROVES: `type Positive is Int { self > 0 }; theorem t(n: Positive) ensures n > 0 proof by auto;` — so the refinement DOES reach the solver as a hypothesis. FAILS: the same theorem with `ensures n * 2 > 0`, `error<E0319>: 1 unproved: n * 2 > 0`, and `proof by smt` and `proof by unfold` fail identically. No user function is involved: multiplying the refined variable by a literal is already past what the SMT encoding carries. FOUND VIA a function (`double(n) > 0` in `examples/showcase.vr`), which made it look like a function-unfolding gap; the inline probe shows unfolding is not the issue. CORRECTING THIS ROW'S OWN DIAGNOSIS, by reading rather than probing further: the refinement does NOT reach the solver. `vcgen.rs`'s `refinement_predicate_formula` matches `TypeKind::Refined` — the SYNTACTICALLY refined annotation `m: Int{> 0}` — and returns `None` for anything else, so a parameter written with a NAMED refinement (`n: Positive`) contributes no hypothesis at all. That also explains the shape of the symptom: if the hypothesis were present, `n * 2 > 0` would follow trivially; if it were simply missing, `n > 0` would fail too. `n > 0` passing while its consequences do not is what a SYNTACTIC discharge looks like, not a solver one. SAME DEFECT, ALREADY FIXED ON THE OTHER SIDE: `verum_vbc`'s `type_refinements` map exists because the runtime-assert emitters had exactly this hole — its comment reads "a binding or parameter written with the alias matched nothing and the assert was never emitted … the predicate is present at type-check time and lost before codegen" (T1063). The verification half needs the same map: resolve a named type to its declared refinement before asking for the formula. NOT ATTEMPTED HERE — five other fixes were awaiting verification and a sixth unverified change would have made the batch unreadable. | open |
| A92 | **THE WEFT OVERVIEW'S REST EXAMPLE IS WRITTEN AGAINST AN API `core/net/weft` DOES NOT HAVE.** | P2 | Measured 2026-09-05 name by name against the source. `Router` has `new / route / get / post / put / patch / delete / nest / fallback / match_request` and NO `.layer` — middleware is `ServiceBuilder`'s (`core/net/weft/service.vr:238`), so the example's `Router.new().route(...).layer(...).layer(...)` chain cannot typecheck. `Json` is a TYPE (`json_extractor.vr`), not a constructor function, so `Ok(Json(u))` is `not a function`. `supervisor_config` and `PostgresDatabase` appear nowhere in `core/`. `Response` is built by the free `resp_*` helpers, which is the one thing fixed here — the `{ /* ... */ }` stub is now a real `into_response`. SCOPE, RE-MEASURED 2026-09-05 by asking WHICH RECEIVER each call hangs off — the earlier count did not: `.layer(` appears 25 times across 7 pages, and MOST are already correct, on `ServiceBuilder` (backpressure.md, service.md). The defective ones are the five chains rooted at `Router.new()` (overview.md, router.md ×2, spiffe.md) plus one at `Service.builder()`, which does not exist either — `Service` is a PROTOCOL (`core/net/weft/service.vr:81`), not a type with a constructor. AND THE CORRECT SPELLING IS NOT A GUESS: the library documents it in its own source, `core/net/weft/timeout.vr` — `let svc = ServiceBuilder.new(router).layer(TimeoutLayer.ms(5000)).build();` — so `Router.new()…​.layer(…)` becomes `ServiceBuilder.new(Router.new()…).layer(…).build()` mechanically. DONE 2026-09-05 for all five: `overview.md`, `router.md` (two chains), `spiffe.md` and `proxy.md`, the last of which called `Service.builder()` on a PROTOCOL and ended with a `.service(handler)` that is not `ServiceBuilder`'s API either — it now wraps the handler and `build()`s, and its mount line brings `ServiceBuilder` in. RE-MEASURED after the edit: ZERO `.layer(` chains on the website are rooted at anything but `ServiceBuilder` — the first re-measurement said zero too and was WRONG, because its walk back to the chain head stopped at a COMMENT line and read `.nest("/admin", admin)` as the head; counting comment and blank lines as continuations found one more, on the same page. A walker that skips only the lines it expects reports the answer it expected. The glob `mount core.net.weft.*` reaches it because `core/net/weft/mod.vr` carries `public mount .service.*;`. What IS a design question, and stays open, is the REST example's `Json(u)` constructor, `supervisor_config` and `PostgresDatabase`, none of which exist anywhere in `core/`. THE PARSE SIDE IS DONE — all six blocks on the page parse. THE SEMANTIC SIDE IS DONE TOO, 2026-09-06, name by name against the source. `Json` DOES exist — `public type Json<T> is { inner: T };` at `core/net/weft/json_extractor.vr:408` — so `Result<Json<User>, ApiError>` was right all along and only the CALL was wrong: it is a record, and the library spells it `Json { inner: value }` one line below its own declaration (`:425`). The row's earlier reading, that `Json` is a type and therefore `Json(u)` is `not a function`, was half right and half an artefact of a probe that searched `^public type Json is` and never asked for the GENERIC form. `supervisor_config()` is replaced by `SupervisorConfig.one_for_one("api")` (`core/runtime/supervisor.vr:723`), and `PostgresDatabase.connect(url)` by `PgAdapter.connect(&PgConfig.new(host, port, user, password, database))` (`adapter.vr:45`, `config.vr:133`), both re-exported at `core.database.postgres`. POPULATION, not just the named page: `PostgresDatabase` had 3 hits in 2 files (`language/context-system.md` carried two more) and the `Json(x)` call shape 2 more (`weft/error.md`, `weft/handler.md`) — all fixed, and a re-count reports zero of either. TWO MORE DEFECTS FELL OUT OF RE-CHECKING THE BLOCK, both verified against source: `resp_bad_request(&m)` takes NO arguments (`core/net/weft/response_ext.vr:75`) and now attaches its body with `resp_with_body_text`; and `WeftApp.new(app).bind(addr)` returns `Result<Server<H>, Text>` (`app.vr:102`) so `.serve()` cannot hang off it directly. CORRECTED 2026-09-06, and the correction is measured: the block's two `Cannot await non-future type: Result<…, SupervisorError>` diagnostics were NOT a compiler-side gap about `supervise` — they were CASCADES from the failing `serve` call in the closure above them. Rewriting `bind(addr)?.serve()` as a split `let` took the block from 4 coded errors to 1, and the survivor is `unbound variable: find_user`, which the example's own comment declares is the reader's helper. The `?.` was mine, added earlier the same day while closing this row's first half, and it was wrong for the reason A95 records: `?.` is the OPTIONAL-CHAINING operator, resolved through `Maybe` and a silent no-op on a `Result`. The claim that `supervise`'s asyncness was the problem is withdrawn. A COMPANION GAP, measured while checking the mounts: `SupervisorConfig` is the parameter type of the public `SupervisorHandle.root` and `core/runtime/mod.vr` does not re-export it, so a caller must reach into the file submodule — the example's mount line says so in place. | FIXED (docs) |
| A93 | **FORTY-SIX COMMITS THIS REGISTER CITES ARE ON NO BRANCH.** | P3 | Measured 2026-09-06 with `git merge-base --is-ancestor <sha> main` over every sha-like token in the file: 112 tokens, 46 of them naming real commits that are reachable from NO branch — dangling objects, several dated June and July, alive only in the reflog. A row that says "fixed in 34129a636" and points at a commit the project no longer has is worse than a row with no citation: the reader believes there is something to look at, and spends a `git show` finding out there is not. CAUSE, not guessed: rebasing. A commit is cited while it sits on a branch; the branch is rebased onto main; the citation keeps the OLD sha, which dies when the reflog expires. THE FIRST COUNT SAID FIFTEEN and was wrong for a reason worth stating — it was taken over the `A`-rows alone, which are a third of the file; the gate reads the whole register. GATED 2026-09-06: `scripts/ci/check_register_shas_are_reachable.py`, in `gates-source`, which CI already runs. A ratchet at 46, not zero: recovering what each dead sha became is 46 separate investigations, while stopping the population growing is one script. Its `--self-test` pins the token filter in both polarities — a decimal count and a hex constant like the nil sentinel must NOT read as commits. CONSEQUENCE MEASURED, not assumed: 31 rows cite at least one dangling commit, and only TWO of those are marked CLOSED — C2 and C4. Both were re-verified independently of their citations and both HOLD. C2 ("44 broken mounts -> 0"): `check_mount_group_integrity.py` reports 10431 files with no group left open, and `check_test_mounts_resolve.py` reports the ratchet holding at 839 known-unresolved with none new. C4 ("intrinsics-side alias decls deleted, Raw* canonical"): `core/intrinsics/runtime/async_ops.vr` declares `RawJoinHandleOpaque` and `RawAllocHandle` while `core/runtime/async_ops.vr` keeps the bare `JoinHandleOpaque` and `AllocHandle` — exactly the layer-pair convention `crates/verum_compiler/tests/stdlib_unique_type_names.rs` documents ("`Raw<Type>` for the FFI-side"), so the duplicate bare declaration on the intrinsics side is indeed gone. So the 46 are a DOCUMENTATION defect — stale pointers — and not, as far as the closed rows go, false closures. The other 29 rows citing dangling commits are open rows whose citations are evidence for a measurement, not for a fix. CLOSED 2026-09-06 — the deliverable is the GATE, not a repair of the 46. They cannot be repaired: three were checked against every branch and against main's log by subject, and neither lookup finds them, so the commits are gone with the reflog rather than merely renamed. What the row prevents is the NEXT one: `scripts/ci/check_register_shas_are_reachable.py` runs `git merge-base --is-ancestor` over every sha-like token in the file and fails above the 46 it inherited, with the token filter pinned in both polarities so a decimal count ("15 files") and a hex constant (`0x7ff9000000000000`) are not read as commits while two real shas on one line are both found. | FIXED (gate) |
| A94 | **THE STDLIB'S PRIVATE DECLARATIONS ARE GLOBALLY VISIBLE IN EVERY USER PROGRAM: 302 CONSTANTS AND 202 TYPES, WITH NO `mount`.** Measured 2026-09-06 by `verum check` on single-line files carrying no `mount` line. A file that is only `fn main() -> Bool { return K; }` gets far enough to report `expected 'Bool', found '[UInt64]'` — `K` being `core/hash/crypto/sha512.vr:83`'s `const K: [UInt64; 80]`, declared WITHOUT `public`. `H_INIT` from the same file behaves identically, and a sample of 8 private constants drawn from 7 unrelated modules (`term/widget/canvas`, `database/sqlite/native/builtins/dispatch`, `compress/mod_gzip`, `net/http_parser` twice, `net/quic/transport_params`, `net/dns`, `database/sqlite/error`) is 8 of 8 visible, and none of the 8 has a `public const` twin anywhere in `core`. Census over `core/`: 332 distinct private constant names of which **302 have no public twin**, and 202 distinct private type names. TYPES LEAK THE SAME WAY, with a two-sided control that separates the answer from the instrument: `let x: ZZQNoSuchType = 0;` gives `error<E101>: type not found`, `let x: Text = 0;` gives a type mismatch naming `Text`, and the private `AliasEntry` and `AeadKind` both give type mismatches naming them — `AeadKind`'s printing its whole variant list. FUNCTIONS DO NOT LEAK: 4 of 4 sampled private functions (`__fd_close_raw_buf`, `addr_eq`, `annotate_constant_cost`, `apply_merge`) are unbound, while the public `monotonic_nanos` IS visible with no mount — so the function path enforces visibility where the constant and type paths do not, and the repair has a working precedent in-tree rather than needing a new policy. STRUCTURAL CAUSE, read off the definitions: no descriptor in `CoreMetadata` carries visibility. `FunctionDescriptor` (`crates/verum_types/src/core_metadata.rs:630-739`) has `is_const` and no `is_public`; `TypeDescriptor` (212-282), `ProtocolDescriptor` (557-597) and `StaticDescriptor` (609-626) have none either; `FieldDescriptor` (376) is the only descriptor in that file with an `is_public` field. So the eager registration `infer/env.rs`'s `#97` performs — "Register every stdlib const (`is_const = true`) from CoreMetadata as a monomorphic value in the type environment" — has nothing to filter on. A user's own declaration DOES shadow correctly (`const K: Int = 5;` then `return K;` reports `found 'Int'`), so the workaround is to name things distinctly and the blast radius is a polluted namespace rather than a wrong answer. FIXED 2026-09-06. `CoreMetadata`'s `FunctionDescriptor` and `TypeDescriptor` carry an `is_public` field, `#[serde(default = "visible_by_default")]` so an archive baked before the field reads as PUBLIC — the stale-archive default has to be the permissive one, since `false` would hide the whole stdlib from every program built against an older `.vbca`. The flag is NOT read off the VBC descriptor: `visibility` there is never written (its `Default` is `Public` and the one assignment in the tree is unrelated), which is why the archive cannot answer the question at all. The bake reads the SOURCE instead, in the same pass that already walks `core/` for context declarations, and `infer/env.rs`'s const registration plus `ensure_stdlib_type_loaded` skip what it marks. THREE KEYS WERE WRONG BEFORE THE FOURTH WAS RIGHT, and each cost a bake, so the shapes are recorded here rather than re-derived: (1) `module_path` — that is the archive ENTRY (`core.hash.crypto`), never the declaring file (`core.hash.crypto.sha512`); marking matched NOTHING and the fix measured INERT, a matrix of sixteen probes identical to the baseline. (2) `origin_module_path` — carries the file for SOME descriptors and is `None` for others; it fixed TYPES and left CONSTANTS untouched. (3) The pair from the descriptor NAME, which for a constant is fully qualified — measured with a throwaway probe test rather than guessed a third time (`crates/verum_compiler/tests/a94_const_descriptor_probe.rs`, kept as `#[ignore]`d alongside the existing `e401_metadata_probe.rs`). (4) A name-only rule beside it, safe by construction: a name with no `public` declaration anywhere in `core/` has no public meaning to shadow. Both rules are needed — only the pair can tell `core.hash.crypto.sha512.K` (private) from `core.hash.crypto.sha256.K` (`public const`). MEASURED AFTER: 8 of 8 sampled private-only constants hidden, 6 of 6 sampled private-only types hidden, every public probe still visible with its correct type, both controls unchanged, and `mount core.base.panic.{panic_fmt}` still 0 errors. The sharpest single reading is `K`: its type went from `[UInt64]` — the private sha512 table — to `[UInt32]`, the `public const` of the same name in sha256, so the private one is gone and the public one answers. BLAST RADIUS: 80 `core/` files through the pre-fix and post-fix binaries give byte-identical error counts, 0 rows differing — and the binaries are known to disagree elsewhere (the probe matrix), so the zero is a result rather than a property of the instrument. GATE: `cargo test -p verum_types --tests` is 153 suites, 0 failures, with the three new cases in `private_stdlib_names_are_not_published_tests.rs` among them — a public const still published, a private one not, and a descriptor with no `is_public` field reading as public. ONE RESIDUE, and it is a `core/` defect rather than a gap in this fix: `FormatArgs` still resolves, because `core/base/panic.vr` declares it private and then names it in the signature of the PUBLIC `panic_fmt`, which pulls it into scope by a different path. **THAT COUNT WAS WRONG AND IS CORRECTED HERE (T1195, 2026-09-06): ELEVEN private `core/` types are named on the public surface, not one** — `BandwidthFilter`, `Bytes32`, `FormatArgs`, `LineBuf`, `PollReadyFuture`, `RttFilter`, `RuntimeTaskHandle`, `StatefulTransducedIter`, `StdoutGate`, `TaskEntry`, `TransducedIter` — measured by a brace-tracked census that JOINS MULTI-LINE SIGNATURES; the single-line version reported eight and missed `StatefulTransducedIter`, whose forcing return type sits on a continuation line. `FormatArgs` is not the only one in that position; it is the only one that happens to still RESOLVE, and the same declaration shape produces OPPOSITE failures depending on which path reaches the name — probed on a re-baked binary, `FormatArgs` is visible while `TaskEntry`, `StdoutGate` and `TransducedIter` answer `error<E101>: type not found`, identical to a name nothing declares. So a private type on the public surface has UNDEFINED visibility, and the second reading is the expensive one: `Iterator.transduce` is a PUBLIC protocol default returning the private `TransducedIter`, so `l.iter().transduce(x).fold(0, f)` reported `no method named 'fold'` and registry-next stopped compiling — 54 scenes to 0. THE EFFECT ARRIVES WITH THE FIRST RE-BAKE, not with this commit: the flag is written by the stdlib bake, and six consecutive binaries spanning six hours all logged `Stdlib precompile cache HIT (blake3 cce44fb820de7fe1)` and behaved as if A94 had never landed, which made a bisect across them inconclusive by construction. All eleven now carry `public`, each with a line naming the signature that forces it, and the rule is gated at zero by `scripts/ci/check_private_types_off_public_surface.py` in `make gates-source` — calibrated at both ends (11 on the pre-fix tree extracted from HEAD, 0 after) with a two-polarity self-test. THE FUNCTION PATH'S FILTER IS AT `crates/verum_compiler/src/precompile.rs:3099`: the source-scan injector reads `ItemKind::Function(fd)` and `continue`s unless `fd.visibility` is `Visibility::Public`, so a private function never reaches a descriptor at all. That is the precedent the constant and type paths lacked, and it is why the archive-strings probe read inconsistently — `grep -cxF` over `strings` counts whole-line matches only, and it called the private `addr_eq` present and the private `apply_merge` absent. | P1 | probes in this row, each with its stated control; `crates/verum_types/src/core_metadata.rs:212,376,557,609,630`; `crates/verum_types/src/infer/env.rs:331` | FIXED |
| A95 | **`?.` ON A `Result` IS A SILENT NO-OP, AND `e?.m()` THEREFORE LOOKS UP `m` ON THE `Result`.** `?.` is not `?` followed by `.`: the lexer has a `QuestionDot` token (`crates/verum_lexer/src/token.rs:1421`) and the grammar a production of its own (`grammar/verum.ebnf:434`, `optional_chain = '?.'`), parsed as `ExprKind::OptionalChain`. Its typecheck resolves the receiver through the **Maybe** protocol only (`crates/verum_types/src/infer/expr.rs:7053`); on anything else the code comments that "the `?.` is a no-op on the receiver" and "we still need to handle it gracefully", so the method is looked up on the un-unwrapped type. THE OPERATOR IS NOT BROKEN — its DOMAIN is narrower than authors assume, and that positive control was measured 2026-09-06: `type Addr is { city: Text }; type User is { addr: Maybe<Addr> };` with `fn a(u: User) -> Maybe<Text> { return u.addr?.city; }` compiles with ZERO errors. So `?.` does exactly what its production says on a `Maybe`, and every one of the seven `?.`-after-a-field usages on the website is correct. Measured with a three-way control in ONE file, which is what makes the cause unambiguous: `let e = make_text()?.is_empty();` fails with `error<E400>: no method named 'is_empty' found for type 'Result<Text, Text>'`, while `make_text()? .is_empty()` — one space — and `(make_text()?).is_empty()` BOTH compile. Three spellings of the same intent, one token pair apart. An author who means try-then-call gets a diagnostic about the method rather than about the operator, and nothing says `?.` applies to `Maybe` alone. THE STDLIB ALREADY WRITES IT: `core/io/protocols.vr:618` carries `Result.Ok(!self.fill_buf()?.is_empty())` where `fill_buf` returns `IoResult<&[Byte]>` — try-then-call under any reading, optional-chaining under the implementation. It reports nothing only because it sits in a protocol default body, which is A96. Two candidate repairs, and the choice is a language decision rather than a bug fix: make `?.` unwrap `Result` as well as `Maybe`, or diagnose `?.` on a non-`Maybe` receiver by name and point at `? .` — what must NOT stand is the silent degradation, which turns an operator-choice mistake into a method-name mystery. DOCUMENTATION POPULATION, measured 2026-09-06 and split by SENSE because the two readings are both present on the site: `?.` after a plain identifier or field — the `Maybe` sense the implementation actually serves — is 7 occurrences and correct, and `migrating/from-typescript.md` teaches exactly that (`user.address?.city?.name`). `?.` after `.await` or a closing paren — the try-then-call sense — is 7 occurrences in 5 files (`async-concurrency.md`, `cookbook/resilience.md`, `cookbook/file-io.md` ×3, `cookbook/http-client.md`, `weft/overview.md`) and every one of them is the shape this row describes. One of the seven was fixed because it was MINE, added the same day while closing A92: rewriting it as a split `let` took that block from 4 coded errors to 1. The other six are left deliberately — rewriting them to `e? .m()` or `(e?).m()` would teach a spacing trick around a defect, and which way they should read is the language decision this row names. | P1 | probes in this row; `crates/verum_lexer/src/token.rs:1421`; `grammar/verum.ebnf:434`; `crates/verum_types/src/infer/expr.rs:7053`; `core/io/protocols.vr:618` | open |
| A96 | **A PROTOCOL'S DEFAULT METHOD BODY IS NEVER TYPECHECKED — 103 BODIES IN 16 `core/` FILES.** Measured 2026-09-06 with a two-sided control that separates the answer from the instrument. A file whose ONLY content is a protocol with `fn helper(&self) -> Int { return ZZQ_NEVER_DECLARED_NAME; }` compiles with **zero** diagnostics; the identical `return ZZQ_NEVER_DECLARED_NAME;` in a free function in the same tree reports `error<E100>: unbound variable`. Not a missing name lookup, then — the body is not visited at all. CENSUS, brace-tracked so it counts only signatures at depth 1 inside a `type X is protocol { … }` block rather than every `fn … {` in the file (the naive regex said 18121 and was counting impl methods): **103 default bodies across 16 files**, and the counter was controlled against a known answer — it finds `core/io/protocols.vr:617` (`BufRead.has_data_left`), which is exactly the body that hides A95's live instance. So the two rows are one story: a construct that fails everywhere it IS checked sits in the stdlib unreported, and 102 other bodies have never been looked at. THE BLAST RADIUS IS UNKNOWN BY CONSTRUCTION and that is the point — no diagnostic has ever been emitted for any of them, so the tree's silence about protocol defaults is not evidence they are correct. First measurement to take: turn the check on and count what falls out, before deciding what to fix. THE INSTRUMENT IS IN THE TREE 2026-09-06 and is INERT unless asked: `VERUM_CHECK_PROTOCOL_DEFAULTS=1` makes the protocol-registration walk build a `FunctionDecl` carrying the default body — from `method.body` or `default_impl`, whichever holds it — hand it to the same `check_function` an `implement` method goes through, and PRINT what comes back as `[proto-default] <Protocol>::<method> — <error>`. It deliberately does not propagate the error: the point is a count, and an enforcement that aborts registration would change the answer it is trying to measure. `cargo check -p verum_types` is clean with it in place. THE CENSUS RAN 2026-09-06, and it took TWO attempts because the first instrument was on the wrong walk. Three sites read `default_impl`, each carrying its own copy of a "CRITICAL FIX" comment; instrumenting one of them reported 3 lines, and the control — a protocol whose default body is `return ZZQ_NEVER_DECLARED_NAME;` — reported NOTHING, which is how the under-count was caught rather than published. A protocol written `public type P is protocol { … }` flows through a different site; with both instrumented the control fires. THE ANSWER: **153 diagnostics across 23 protocols**, led by `Iterator` (53), `StreamExt` (43), `Semiring` (9), `Ord` (7) and `FutureExt` (6). And the answer is one fact repeated, not 153 defects — **152 of the 153 are the same thing**: 96 are `type inference recursion limit exceeded`, 53 are `MethodNotFound` and EVERY ONE of those 53 is on the receiver type `Self`, plus one `Cannot negate type: Self`. A body checked with no concrete `Self` cannot be typed, so what the count measures is the absence of a `Self` model, not the state of the bodies. That includes the case this row was written around: `BufRead::has_data_left` does appear, and it reports `MethodNotFound` on `Self` rather than the `?.`-on-a-`Result` defect A95 describes. SO THE NEXT STEP IS NAMED, and it is not "fix 153 things": bind `Self` to an abstract type carrying the protocol's own methods before the check can say anything at all. Until then the gate stays opt-in and inert. The corpus is 16 files, identified by the brace-tracked walk in this row, and the run is one loop: `VERUM_CHECK_PROTOCOL_DEFAULTS=1 verum check <file>` over them, counting `[proto-default]` lines. Expect the count to be a floor rather than the truth: a body that fails EARLY may mask what follows it. | P1 | probes in this row; census recipe in this row; `core/io/protocols.vr:617` as the control case | open |
| A97 | **THE SITE TEACHES NAMES THE LIBRARY DOES NOT HAVE, AND NO GATE COULD SEE IT.** A parse gate asks whether a block parses. `PostgresDatabase.connect(&db_url)` parses perfectly and names nothing that exists; so did `supervisor_config()`, `Json(u)` as a call, and `type Database.ReadOnly`. Every one was live while the parse baseline stood at zero, and every one was found BY HAND, one page at a time, against a site of 371 pages and 2790 `verum` blocks. GATED 2026-09-06 by `scripts/ci/check_doc_names_exist.py` (wired into `gates-docs`), which counts capitalised receivers — `Name.method(...)` — that `core/` declares nowhere. FIVE OF THE FIRST SIX NARROWINGS WERE THE INSTRUMENT'S OWN FAULT, each found by reading the sites it named rather than trusting the count, and each is written into the script so it is not re-learned: (1) the declaration pattern must admit modifiers — a first version matched `(public )?(fn\|type\|const)` and reported `serve`, which is declared `async fn`; (2) scope is the CORPUS, not the page — `Http` (8 pages) and `ConsoleLogger` (6) are reader-supplied contexts declared once, by a convention `cookbook/http-client.md` states outright, and counting them would push the site toward duplicating a declaration it deliberately factored out; (3) comments are prose and prose ends sentences — every `KiB.` is "32 KiB.", and that was two fifths of the count; (4) strings are data — `db.query("SELECT ...")` was read as a receiver because of the ellipsis; (5) `core/`'s SUM-TYPE VARIANTS are declarations too — `NetworkError` is `\| NetworkError(Text)` at `core/net/dns.vr:175`, and the variant rule had been applied to doc blocks but not to the library; (6) a section the page MARKS as unshipped is honest documentation of a plan — `testing-tui.md` says "None of this section exists … measured, not guessed", which is this gate's own finding written down before the gate existed, and the marker scopes to the SECTION rather than to the admonition, measured after cutting at the closing `:::` removed 475 characters and left every name behind. THE LARGEST REAL DEFECT FOUND AND FIXED: three pages taught a runtime bootstrap with four fictional links out of five — `Runtime.new(config)` (`core/runtime/mod.vr:511` declares `Runtime` as a UNIT type with statics and no constructor), `RuntimeConfig` (a protocol), `worker_threads` (the field is `async_worker_threads`), and `io_engine(IoEngineKind.IoUring)` (neither name occurs anywhere in the tree). The real entry is `RuntimeBuilder` at `core/async/executor.vr:2234` returning `AsyncRuntime`, which is what owns `block_on`. SECOND CLASS FIXED: two context pages swapped in four dependencies without saying which ship — exactly one did (`NullLogger`, `core/base/log.vr:535`) — and the `layer` example bound three application types as if they were the library's. Baseline 121 pairs at installation, **21** after these, and that 21 is a FLOOR rather than unfinished work: every survivor is a name the example itself owns — test doubles (`FakeDatabase`, `MockResolver`, `RecordingLogger`), `My…` types (`MyError`, `MyAuditLog`), and domain props (`FraudClient`, `HomeScreen`, `SettingsScreen`, `SqlParser`, `Sodium`). Declaring each would make 21 examples self-contained and change nothing about the library, so the gate's remaining value is the RATCHET: a twenty-second name means a page has started teaching something `core/` does not have, which is exactly how all five defects above entered. FIVE MORE FIXED after the row was first written: `RDMA.connect(peer)` on the maths page, where four of the five entries in that sketch were real and the fifth was a CONTEXT under another name (`context RdmaContext`, `core/math/distributed.vr:1039`); `set_missed_tick_behavior(MissedTickBehavior.Skip)`, where `Interval` is real but the behaviour is not configurable — `tick()` REPORTS the number of elapsed periods and the caller chooses; and three whole sections that document absent APIs, now carrying the site's own `:::caution Not shipped` marker with what IS there beside them — `SpiffeClientTransport` (zero occurrences; `HttpClient` is a protocol whose only implementation is inside a `///` comment), the DoH `DnsTransport` (zero occurrences, and `core/net/dns.vr` has no HTTPS reference at all), and the REPL embedding API. The recipe stays: measure the name against `core/`, fix it or mark it, lower the baseline. | P1 | `scripts/ci/check_doc_names_exist.py`; `make check-doc-names-exist` | open |
| A98 | **A PRIVATE STDLIB DECLARATION CAN OWN THE SIMPLE NAME OF A PUBLIC TYPE, AND A94 THEN REFUSES THE PUBLIC ONE.** Measured 2026-09-06. `core/net/quic/stats_prometheus.vr:231` declares a private `type TextBuilder is { parts: List<Text> }`; the public `TextBuilder` is `core/text/builder.vr:77`. A program that mounts the PUBLIC one by path reports `error<E400>: no method named 'is_empty' found for type 'TextBuilder'` — on a type that declares it. `VERUM_TRACE_CTOR=1` names the cause in one line: `ensure-load PRIVATE name=TextBuilder`, followed by `mount-auth 'TextBuilder': qualified 'core.text.builder.TextBuilder' NOT FOUND`. `metadata.types` is keyed by SIMPLE NAME and that slot is FIRST-WINS across the whole archive (`archive_metadata.rs`, `if !simple_slot_was_occupied`), so A94's `if !type_desc.is_public { return; }` read the wrong descriptor and registered NONE of the public type's methods — which is also why the explicit mount looked ignored: the type is never loaded, so there is nothing for the mount to point at. A94's rule is right and is not narrowed; it is about the DECLARATION the user reaches, not about the name, and \"a private type is not the user's to name\" must not become \"a private type's name hides a public homonym\". FIXED at `crates/verum_types/src/infer/core.rs` (PRIVATE-HOMONYM-DOES-NOT-HIDE-PUBLIC-1): when the simple slot holds a private descriptor, recover a PUBLIC one of the same simple name from a qualified key, lexicographically smallest for determinism across bakes; a name with no public declaration anywhere still misses, which is the 182-name case A94 measured. THE EXPOSURE IS THE COLLISION LIST, not this pair: `make check-type-name-collisions` is GREEN at 132 colliding simple names, 68 of them method-bearing, so 68 programs' compilation depended on which homonym owned a hash-map slot — a dependency invisible in the source of both files. FOUND BY a by-example blast-radius sweep, and the same sweep found a SECOND, independent defect: the example called `b.push_str(word)`, which is the PRIVATE quic helper's method (the public builder's is `push`), so it had been written against a type the reader cannot legitimately reach and passed every gate by relying on the homonym. THE ATTRIBUTION TOOK TWO ATTEMPTS AND THE FIRST WAS MINE TO CORRECT: the bracketed window (08:43 works, 11:48 fails) contains A96, which is inert — its whole body sits inside `if std::env::var(\"VERUM_CHECK_PROTOCOL_DEFAULTS\").is_ok()` — and A94's effect arrives not with its commit but with the first stdlib re-bake after it, which is why the window brackets the BAKE rather than the commit. | P0 | `vcs/specs/L0-critical/vbc/e2e/637_a_private_homonym_does_not_hide_a_public_type.vr`; probes in this row; `make check-type-name-collisions` for the 132-name population | FIXED |
